Russia: Yeltsin’s Provincial Tours Viewed
MM2304131996 Moscow KOMSOMOLSKAYA
PRAVDA in Russian 23 Apr 96 p2

{Article by Aleksandr Gamov: "Yeltsin on the Sweet
Quite Unlike Yeltsin in the Office’]

{FBIS Translated Text} This April has perhaps been
for President Yeltsin the most “trip-laden” month in
all his years of rule: Belgorod, Staryy Oskol, Khimki
near Moscow, Krasnodar, Stavropol. Next in line are
Khabarovsk and Almaty and between them a visit to
China.

But in che two months before the elections it ts not
only the speed and altitude of movement of the thinner
and fitter Boris Yeltsin which has changed, but also
the nature of the trips themselves. For instance, the
Belgorod wip arose spontaneously Kiev fell by
the way and B.N. Rapidly “changed his ticket.” The
Krasnodar trip and the Stavropol visit were prepared
in advance but as Nikolay Yegorov, the head of the
presidential staff, “complained” to journalists Yeltsin
had so altered the preliminary route plans that they
were unrecognizable. Here he allegedly grumbled: “In
my opinion I am going not to wear out the seat of my
pants at aktiv meetings but to campaign for President
Yeltsin.” According to the estimates of journalists who
work with him constantly, the correlation between the
president's meetings on the streets and in his “narrow
circle of associates” is now 10:1 while before it was the
other way round.

Yeltsin “on the street” is quite unlike Yeltsin “im the
office and on television.” First, he is not so carefully
groomed. Second, his jacket 1s usually open so that one
can see the fresh heies on his belt: “Look, he really has
lost weight!” Third, when he speaks he does not hesitate
because he is not looking at a piece of paper and even
his famous “shta” and “panmayesh’ [mispronunciations
of Russian words for "what" and “you realize’ | are seen
simply as commas

Something which was not there before — at almost all
his meetings with ordinary people he is the first to turn
the talk to his health. Actually, that it is not letting the
president down and, according to his assurances, will
not let him down, can be judged by the very strenuous
schedule of visits. After a long flight Yeltsin comes
down the gangway on steady feet. immediately gets into
a helicopter or car — and just try keeping up with him
The dinners, to the chagrin of his hospitable hosts, have
become less lavish and extended

But the “flying Yeltsin” is not only showing his voters
a suitable “presidential fitness.” “Yeltsin, give us back
sausage at 2.10 rubles a kilogram'” an elderly Cossack

PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES

woman at the Novo-Mishastovskaya village attacked
him almust with her fists. The president did not recoil
but began to explain “bravely” that sausage can no
longer be brought back but pensions can and should
be increased. In recent years he did not descend to such
trivia.” Or was it that he did not rise to therm”

How the president hurried, what plain and annoyance
were reflected in his eyes when on Krasnodar’s cen-
tral square a front line veteran in front of an enor-
mous crowd refused to shake Boris Nikolayevich’s out-
stretched hand and then suggested that he renounce the
presidency and retire on pension. Yeltsin had never
found himself in such a “fix.” As soon as he had gone
the veteran introduced himself: “Aleksey Fedorovich
Zhikhorev.” To the question, why did he snub the pres-
ident, he replied: “His hands are covered in tears and
blood.

I can attest that Boris Nikolayevich heard with his
own ears people chanting: “Yeltsin's gang sh
on trial.” | saw with my own eyes among the fluttering
red flags communist slogans like “Yeltsin means the
war in Chechnya. Dewn with the war'” One can only
guess what his feelings were. But he has now abandoned
mass meetings on the streets which give him himself
and Aleksandr Korzhakov's security service 4 mass of
problems

uld po

Moreover, during his recent visit to Budennovsk he even
introduced an innovation. His bodyguards were tollow
ing the president along the street with a microphone and
a loudspeaker and it was not only the journalisis milling
nearby but also the entire surrounding area which heard
voters “attacking the president” and Yelisin reassuring
them. Actually, the president's loudspeaker magnified
with equal loudness the delighted cries of Yeltsin's sup-
porters but it seemed that affected him less

Evidently Boris Nikolayevich will, at least until the
elections, have to carry this “public cross
Russian streets. As we know the Kremlin is now shaping
a new image of presidential candidate Yeltsin. They
have already established the “rungs” by climbing which
the incumbent head of state can, his advisers believe,
remain in power. They include the effective indexing
and prompt payment of wages — both for workers in
budget-funded enterprises and workers 1n enterprises of
other forms of ownership — the introduction of order in
pensions, “targeted” support for the poor, the lowering
of the unemployment levei, and the
strikes. And, of course, the peaceful solution of the
urgent Chechen problem

through the

anticipation !

The president has set himself a difficult task: returnin;
trust in the “bright future of reforms to as many
as possible of his former supporters, in

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES

humiliated by the present regime. So he cannot manage
without “street politics.” As he himself admitted at one
meeting in Belgorod: "I have come to look people in
the eyes and speak frankly

We can already note that the action “to instill trust” is
having some success, as is borne out by the rise in the
president's popularity ratings in the regions which he
has already visited. Perhaps that is also explained by the
fact that Yeltsin is, as he himself has put it, “not going tc
the voters empty-handed.” You need a combine? You
don’t? Then what go you need? You need to provide
amenities for your farm? Fine, | sb.ll provide them....”
But is it the president's job to hand out combines and
provide amenities for farms?

And what are these visits to the people giving the
president? During one trip I put that question to him
directly. "When | meet with ordinary people irrespective
of whether I am cursed or thanked for something, I have
a keener sense of Russia,” Boris Nikolayevich replied
I realize that things are hard for many people now. At
the same time I can see that people are not standing idle,
they are working and raising their children.... And they
are even smiling — bright, kindly smiles. That instills
the conviction that for Russia and for each one of us all
is not yet lost. We are being reborn...

One more factor explaining why the “new Yeltsin” has
appeared at the election -tarting block. It is obviously
some inner feeling that is telling Boris Nikolayevich
that his closest entourage’s concerted chanting of “only
Yeltsin for President” will not have the “deciding vote”
at the forthcoming elections. So he has set off on a tour
of Russia to extend a hand to those who so far have no
intention of shaking it.

Russia: Zyuganov Notes Preparations for Formal
Handover of Power

LD2304141796 Moscow Russian Television Network

[From the “Vesti” newscast]

[FBIS Translated Text} At a meeting with journalists
today [Communist Party leader] Gennadiy Zyuganov
said: If we can add to the 22 million votes cast for
us in December, there is a chance that we will not just
get through the first round of voting.

It was announced that the news conference would deal
with the outcome of the summit and nuclear safety
issues, but the communists’ leader was very much more
interested in future success in the presidential elections:
[video shows Zyuganov addressing news conference]

[begin Zyuganov recording] We have to work out a
procedure which will enable a proper handover of

24 April 1996

power. A draft procedure for the handover of power has
been prepared, a draft law which will be submitted this
week to the council of the State Duma. [end recording);

The main moving forces in the future policy of the
communists will be sovereign power [derzhavnost| and
social justice. In Zyuganov's opinion, these ideas wil!
attract many supporters. Talks are proceeding, he said,
evea with neonle he had not reckoned on. There are also
his ioyal comrades: Working Russia, the Agrarians, and
the trade unions. Two other leading political movements
in the country — the Democratic Movement and the
Central-Conservative movement — are less numerous
[video cuts back to Zyuganov at news conference]

{begin Zyuganov recording] There is one regular feature
observed in all the elections we have had so far: prior
to the elections the democrats’ ratings rise sharply,
regardless of their position or status, and after the
elections they fall sharply back again. [end recording]

Russia: Zyuganov’s Campaign Tactics Pondered

MM2404101196 Moscow SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA
in Russian 20 Apr 96 pl

{Article by Aleksandr Frolov under the
Opinion” rubric: “Regarding Tactics” ]

Observer's

{[FBIS Translated Text} Two months before the presi-
dential elections a qualitatively new situation has taken
shape in Russia’s political life. Its essence is that soci-
ety has been overwhelmed by an avalanche of presiden-
tial initiatives and edicts which constitute nothing other

This report may contain copynghted matenal. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

than implementation of the demands which the opposi-
tion advancej in the December State Duma elections,
thereby winning. Indeed:

Chubays and Kozyrev — the most odious figures, who
for five years had enjoyed the reputation of being totally
unsinkable and of whom it was evea said (and not for
nothing) that their appointment was approved by no
means in the Moscow Kremlin, but in the Washington
White House — have been dismissed. The start of
a fight against corruption has been revealed. Several
major officials headed by [former General Prosecutor]
[lyushenko are under investigation.

Measures are being taken to repay the state’s wage and
pension debts. Compensation payments for pensioners
have been introduced, and an initiative on indexing
pensions has been aired. A promise has been made to
restore the population's savings, which were devalued
as a result of the price liberalization. Similar steps to
help deceived investors are also promised.

A number of protectionist measures in support of Rus-
sian commodity producers have been introduced. As-
sistance is promised to the peasantry, high-technology
production facilities in the military-industrial complex,
basic science, education, and culture.

Attempts are being made to end the war in Chechnya
by resuming the negotiating process. A withdrawal of
troops, albeit a symbolic one, from areas inhabited
predominantly by Russians has been begun.

A treaty between four republics on deepening integra-
tion and the treaty with Belarus on creating the SSR
[Community of Sovereign Republics] have been con-
cluded. The victory banner, in effect the Soviet state
flag, has been granted equal status with the Russian state
flag.

Other surprises cannot be ruled out either. Logically, as
election day approaches we can expect further oxtremely
radical steps. In particular, proposals to revise some
results of privatization are very likely. Internal Affairs
Minister Kulikov has floated a trial balloon about the
possibility of nationalizing major oil and gas companies
and commercial banks. Finally, at the last moment there
may be a proposal to introduce into the constitution
amendments aimed at slightly expanding the powers of
the legislative branch of power.

The situation is not so much unexpected as not entirely
familiar so far as the opposition is concerned. The last
time something similar was to be observed was three
years ago, when Yeltsin was preparing for the resolute
fight for power and the dissolution of the soviets. There
was a similar avalanche of edicts, a similar eruption
of promises preempting possible actions by parliament.

PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES

And exactly the same “preparatory bombardment” of
rivals.

On the one hand the president is implementing a
number of points of the opposition program, and on
the other he is declaring that many of its prominent
figures (Lukyanov, Varennikov, Ryzhkov) should be in
Matrosskaya Tishina jail rather than the State Duma.
This is a "slip of the tongue” that reveals more about
the president's true intentions than any number of bland
speeches. Ex erience shows that Yeltsin never makes
such “slips” by accident. This is his “hallmark” —
whipping up tension by any availabie means. He is using
his campaign events not so much to achieve an honest
victory in the elections as to create a confrontational
psychological atmosphere conducive to all sorts of
adventures. An attempt is being made even betore the
elections to organize in his own support a “universal
expression of the people's will” which he could rely on
in order somehow to “avoid” the elections.

The question arises: What should be our view of what
is happening? According to the schemes of Yeltsin's
campaign organizers, all this is suppose to cause
confusion in the opposition ranks. The servile jress 1s
already exulting that the president is knocking “trump
cards” one after another out of the hands of his main
rival. The hope is clearly that the reaction will be
inappropriate.

In order to ensure that this hope is not vindicated, it 1s
important to prevent two main possible errors. We must
not give way to very powerful inertia and respond to any
actions by the regime solely in harshly negative terms

in a desire to immediately “expose” and “brand” them
It would also be a serious blunder to lament, still less
publicly complain that the government is taking over
opposition slogans.

I think that the time has now come to act differently
The need for a change of tactics emerged clearly as
far back as the end of last year in connection with
the serious objective change in the opposition’s status
within society.

Before December people's-patriotic forces called them-
selves (and were indeed) the implacable opposition
This term embraces a very great deal, but neverthe-
less almost the most essential feature of the implacable
Opposition is that it declines and does not bear any
responsibility for the consequences of the state author-
ities’ actions. It is preoccupied primarily with the prob-
lem of politically and organizationally strengthening its
own ranks and gathering strength.

The substantial success in the State Duma elections
sharply changed the situation. When it won around half

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES

of the deputies’ seats the opposition became in the eyes
of the people te some extent an “inbuilt element” of
power and thereby lost the political and moral nght
to continue to decline responsibiiity for the situation
in the country. Hence the new tactics and the new
self-description adopted by people’s-patriotic forces: the
responsible opposiiion —- in contrast to irresponsible
opposition and irresponsible power in the shape of the
president and the government.

The essence of the new tactic, in a nutshell, is that
henceforth the opposition assumes a share of the respon-
sibility for everything that is happening in the country
and does not bide its time until it holds executive power
but right now, even given the extreme lack of potential
enjoyed by the State Duma, will use that potential in
order to implement its own election promises.

Everything begins to look different in the light of the
above. The president's recent actions merely confirm
the correctness and validity of the opposition’ s demands
They become evidence that the opposition has sufficient
strength and potential to substantially influence state
policy. The positive shifts that are taking place are
entirely down to the opposition and are the result of
vigorous pressure on the president and the government.
Thus what was conceived as an argument in favor of
Yeltsin's reelection is in fact the main election trump
card of G. Zyuganov, the presidential candidate of
people's patriotic forces. He is not merely promising
but ensuring implementation of his promises.

The hysteria kicked up over the State Duma’s repeal
of the Supreme Soviet decree on denouncing the 1922
treaty on the formauon of the USSR becomes under-
standable in this context. The Duma preempted the pres-
ident’s integration initiatives, so it had to be discred-
ited at all costs. But the hysteria was instantly halted as
soon as it became clear that it was merely serving to
propagandize the stance of the people’s-patriotic forces
and was placing the president in the awkward position
of trying to catch up. It is clearly for the same reason
that top officials are still maintaining total silence about
the opposition’s fundamentally important initiatives on
checking the legality and economic consequences of pri-
vatization and on submitting amendments to the consti-
tution. This enables us to assume that proposals in this
sphere may soon be forthcoming from the presidential
side.

It is now a matter of making correct use of the potential
advantage. To this end the center of gravity of campaign
tactics must be focused not on new promises but on
demonstrating that promises have already begun to be
kept. As for the fact that the president is seizing the
initiative from the opposition, this certainly does not

FBIS-SOV -96-080
24 April 1996

frighten us. After all, the most important thing ts that
they are implemented. Not only do we have no intention
of preventing this, we will offer legislative support. But
we greatly doubt whether our opponents will be able to
implement the proclaimed aims consistently and to any
substantial extent

First, these are mainly steps dictated by campaign
expediency — in other words, they are clearly grudging
and temporary. There are no guarantees that everything
will not be reversed if Yeltsin 1s reelected for a second
term. Rather, it is certain that this will indeed happen
and the promises will be forgotten, as has already
happened repeatedly. It can also be assumed almost
with certainty that Yeltsin will seek to dissolve the
opposition-minded Duma. Therefore we must elect a
president representing the parliamentary majority and
acting in a coordinated fashion with it to consistently
implement the new course.

Second, many of the measures that are being imple-
mented, which require very great expenditure, do not
rest on any firm socioeconomic foundation. The foun
dations of the state's economic policy remain fallacious
The current large-scale disbursements are being made
by getting into deh, cutting investment, and playing an
adventurist game of financial “pyramids.” So a tremen
dous collapse lies in store unless the changes that are
under way affect deeper strata of the economy. Only a
new people's power can do this. So a change of head ot
State Is necessary precisely in order to ensure that these
positive trends are not only politically guaranteed but
also economically bolstered

Third, the incontestably useful and necessary
toward integration of the former Union republics are
nevertheless a backward step even compared with the
letter of the agreements on forming the CIS, which
envisaged a considerably higher level of unity than the
SIG” (Community of Integrated States} and the “SSR

After all, they proclaimed the preservation of a single
economic area, a single currency, common borders, joint
armed forces, and much else besides. Effectively what
was proclaimed was the preservation of the Umon
but...without the Union center. However, all this exists
only on paper. So the signing of the new agreements
against the background of the inability to fulfill the
existing ones gives the impression of mere window
dressing

‘ eps

Fourth and last, the initiatives on settling the Chechea
conflict contain nothing new from the military-political
viewpoint and still totally ignore the socimeconomiK
aspects of the problem. But unless the reasons why
Chechnya became a criminal free economic zone are
elirunated, unless the channels for illegal trafficking of

This report may contain copynghted matenal Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV-96-059
24 April 1996

weapons, drugs, and duty-free goods via the Chechen
black hole” are bloc’sed — unless this is done the
politicians and the military will be able to achieve
nothing. The roots of the war are to be found tar beyond
Chechnya, including in Moscow. And in order to really
sever them, once again we need a change of president
and government.

Suraming up the above, I want to stress once again that
the optimum line in the opposition’s mass political work
could de the following. There is no reason to deny the
slight positive trends that have emerged in government
policy since the start of the secoed Duma’s work. But
at the same time it must be persistently explained that
these are the result of the active state activity of the
responsible opposition both in parliament and outside
it. But they are clearly grudging, half-hearted, and
inconsistent, and the poor social and economic validity
of these steps is clear. The overall conclusion ts this:
The more consistent implementation and consolidation
of fae positive tends requires that executive power be
placed in the hands of those who are their effective
initiators

Our opponents may object that, yes, many mistakes and
excesses have been allowed to occur, but since Boris
Nikolayevich “has himself begun” at long last to recufy
them, he should not be prevented from continuing this
noble task. He has started it, so he should be allowed
to complete the job. Shumeyko’s RNK [Reforms-New
Policy] movement, for instance, bases ail its arguments
on this thesis

But does Yeltsin deserve to be trusted” In my view, the
five-year history of his “reign” has long since made that
a purely rhetorical question

[FBIS Transiated Text] Chelyabinsk — The people of
Chelyabinsk do not particularly favor the Communist
Party of the Russian Federation [CPRF]: The recent
State Duma elections were confirmation of that. Back
then the Communists ended up in fourth place. Perhaps
that 1s why the CPRF leader has paid a second visit to
Chelyatnsk, this time as a presidential candidate

Gennadiy Zyuganov's press conference was held in the
former CPSU Oblast Commutice Bureau auditorium,
where the oblast duma now holds its sessions. He sat
im the seat which was occupied for years by the first

PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES

secretaries. Just like old times. Only the Russian tncolor
reminded us of the present.

The journalists sincerely attempted to get to the bottom
of the specific plans making up the candidate's very un-
specific electoral platform. But contradictions kept peep-
ing out from under the veil of generous promises and
slogans, and logically it did not all hang together. Ful-
minating against the current authorities, G. Zyuganov
posed as a defender of the people's interesis and a pro-
tector of the poor and the orphaned. He tried to convince
people that, if only they would vote for him, a “bright
future” would be around the corner. Pensions would be
higher, as would grants, wages....

It was Clear from these promises that the printing presses
would be switched on again and people would have
to cart away their money in sacks on pay day. Just
as happened after the revolution. This was confirmed
by Gennadiy Andreyevich himself: “If pensions, grants,
and wages were paid today at the average consumer
level, store shelves would empty immediately.” It turns
out that under Zyuganov, who would be all things to all
men in carrying out his election promises, the shelves
would be empty and money would be worthless

Gennadiy Andreyevich proudly announced that he now
has the support of 150 parties and movements. But he
was unable to say what would happen to his ideas when
he came to power. Because all these parties, to use
Anpilov’s expression, would start “beating up” on him
Would not all his energies be squandered on infightung?
After all, these parties are dominated by people whose
programs are based purely on sloganeering and who
have no time for economics

Russia: Zhirinovskiy Denies Campaign Cooperation
With Zyuganov

LD2304173096 Moscow INTERFAX in English

1459 GMT 23 Apr 96

[FBIS Transcribed Text} MOSCOW, April 23 (Interfax)
— Russian Liberal Democratic Party [LDPR] leader
Viadimir Zhirinovskiy has refuted reports that he and
Communist Party leader Gennadiy Zyuganov are plan-
ning closer cooperation in the election canipaign. Inter-
fax reported such plans earlier Monday, citing an in-
formed source in LDPR.

‘The LDPR has always been and remains an indepen-
dent political force which has spoken both against com-
munists and democrats during the six years Jf its exis-
tence,” Zhirinovskiy wold an Interfax parliamentary cor-
respondent in the State Duma.

We have never held any talks with the Communists
about anything. Neither we are going to speak with them
about the formation of any blocs in the run-up to the
presidential elections,” he said.

He also refuted media reports that under certain condi-
tions he was prepared to give his votes to the incumbent
president.

Zhirinovskiy sees two possible reasons behind such
attempts to “mislead the public” about his pre-election
contacts with Zyuganov.

Either it is an attempt to nudge us toward forming a
union with the Communist Party, which is too shy to
contact us directly, or the president's enemies are trying
to distress him this way before his visit to China: you
leave, and meanwhile an anti-Yeltsin front is going to
strengthen,” Zhirinovskiy said.

Russia: ‘Third Force’ To Choose Single
Presidential Candidate

1038 GMT 23 Apr 96

{By Lyudmila Aleksandrova}

[FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW April 23 (Itar-
Tass) — Three Russian presidential hopefuls, known
as the “third force” which opposes both President Boris
Yeltsin and communist candidate Gennadiy Zyuganov,
have agreed to name only one of them as a presidential
candidate one month before June 16 elections.

We have agreed to determine one month before elec-
tions who (of us) is the most popular’. eye surgeon
Svyatoslav Fedorov told Tass on Tuesday

PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES

Besides Fedorov, the “third force” includes liberal
reformist Grigoriy Yavtinskiy and hard rule advocate
and general Aleksandr Lebed

“If Grigoriy Yavlinskiy wins according to the raung, he
would be the candidate, if Aleksandr Lebed does he
will", Fedorov said

All the three have been already registered as presidential
candidates and, if their estimated number of votes 1s
united, their single candidate get around 20 per cent of
support.

Fedorov said he would step down “with a sigh of relief
and “with satisfaction”. The eye surgeon would then
“go into the shade or agree to occupy less important
positions in the executive bodies

Fedorov also said the three candidates had agreed to
adopt a joint Charter of economic reforms for the
upcoming two years.

Fedorov said he trusts the popularity ratings published
in the press, however, the three candidates are going to
hold their own research with the help of independent
analysts.

Russia: Babichey Comments on NDR Election

Home" [NDR] movement will assemble for their third
congress. What will be the main issue ai the congress’
I put this question to Vladimir Babichev, head of the
"Russia Is Our Home” Executive Committee

{[Babichev] First and foremost, the congress will discuss
active, all-embracing participation by our movement in
the presidential election campaign, with one specific
goal — that of doing everything in our power to
ensure victory for Boris Nikolayevich Yeltsin. And the
movement does have potential — and plenty of 1

Remember the labels that were pinned on the NDR
when the movement was just being formed: from

crushing “homebodies” (“domushniki"]. The fate of a
stillborn child, a crushing defeat in the State Duma
elections, was predicted for “Our Home.” Time has
arranged things in its own way. Many parties and
movements of the democratic opposition have receded
iito the deep political shadows, or even into oblivion,
while the NDR has formed its own faction in the lower
chamber of parliament and the number of its supporters
is growing, and by no means only within the Garden
Ring [i.e. in inner Moscow]. [Babichev ends]

...We met with V. Babichev immediately after the NDR
Executive Committee session which set the date for the
movement's congress — 25 April — and which also
discussed two very important questions: the creation of
a youth organization within the movement and ways
of developing the NDR's cooperation with the trade
unions. "No movement can count on success without
the support of young people,” V. Babichev says. “And
we intend to cooperate closely with the trade unions in
the Russian presidential election campaign.”

Today the NDR has become an integral part of the All-
Russia Movement for Public Support for Boris Yeltsin.
A logical step, but still: Could one movement thus
become dissolved within the other? Viadimir Babichev

categorically disagrees with this way of looking at the
matter:

“We have occupied a very specific niche within the
new movement. And we did not join for the sake
of numbers and keeping score. We have our own
tasks.... No, it is not right to speak about some kind of
dissolution. Moreover, the Yeltsin support movement
is a t(mporary organization which is necessary and
important specifically for the present stage.”

It is well known that Boris Yeltsin's election platform
will be published in May. NDR experts were among
those taking part in drafting its basic concept. There cer-
tainly is no shortage of bright people in the movement.
It is another matter whether there are common points
and differences between the present presidential plat-
form and the NDR's election program for the Decem-
ber State Duma elections. My interviewee stresses: “We
are talking about different elections and correspondingly
different documents and emphases — political and eco-
nomic. But there are common goals too — the con-
tunuation of reforms, stabilization in society, and, most
important, to ensure that our candidate wins, and stays
on as president of all Russians.

"By choosing Boris Yeltsin we are saying: There
is NO more experienced, balanced politician in our
homeland.” V. Babichev goes on. “Times now are
hard, transitional, and it is better not to change the
president in midstream. The word ‘stability’ is a key

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

word in the NDR program and in the president's as
yet unpublished platform, although Boris Nikolayevich
himself has already ‘permitted’ a glance at the text by
announcing at the congress of his supporters the 10
theses and 10 main areas of work.

"] think the necessary tone and words of conviction will
be found in this platform, and these will be followed
by concrete action even before the election, so as to
alleviate the lot of the most deprived section of Russians
who have found themselves in effect below the poverty
line,” V. Babichev believes. “These are the people who
will turn out to vote, and all the rest will see that they
have shown concern for their nearest and dearest. Today
the whole of economic policy and the reformist course
must be turned not toward the Russian person in general,
but specifically toward the person whom we have lost
along the path of reforms. That is the person who must
be raised up, encouraged, and instilled with confidence,
using material means among others. Otherwise nothing
will work.”

Confidence in tomorrow permeates the annual Message
of the Russian Federation President to the Federal
Assembly. How is it being implemented in practice,
what is the NDR’s role in this? Virtually every section
of the Message has a concrete economic, management,
and financial charge.

“Since they call us the ‘party of power,’" my interviewee
explains — and he is, let me remind you, the leader of
the Russian Federation Government apparatus — “we,
as the party of the executive power, must translate the
Message into practical terms. We studied the documeni
and extracted concrete sections from it. That amounts to
more than SO draft edicts, some 30 government decrees,
and nearly 15 draft laws. [no closing quotemarks as
published]

These must be “launched” as early as April-May.
And every edict or decree brings with it a whole
slew of documents. So tackling the implementation
of the Message 1s not only honorable, but extremely
troublesome. But nobody is going to complain, because
it is clear that it is ultimately a question of real changes
in the Russians” lives

Nonetheless, reasonable questions may arise among our
readers — and not only them. True, it is noteworthy
that edicts and resolutions are coming out in which the
ordinary working person, the veteran, and the invalid
who has lost his health are put first. But where is the
money to come from for these pensions and wages ‘or
budget-funded employees? Does the NDR take part,
as the “party of power.” in public monitoring of the
implementation of these vitally important matters?

This report may contain copynghted matenal Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

This is what I learned from the leader of the NDR Ex-
ecutive Committee. There is monitoring — at govern-
ment level. As for resources, a significant proportion
of the aforementioned documents have the budget to
back them. It is a peculiarity of the present situation in
the Russian economy, V. Babichev explains, that in the
second half of last year and this year for the first time
the country’s budget has had any possibility of giving
people something. Normal budget funding has finally
come to pass, a real reserve fund, although there are
still plenty of hitches. Take, for instance, the situation
regarding receipt of taxes. Nonetheless the government
is not now racking its brains over how to help a spe-
cific university, theater, school, etc. After all, we were
deprived of all that in 1992, 93, °94! The light appeared
at the end of the tunnel, let us remember, only in 1995.

Envoys of “Our Home” will come to their congress next
Thursday from all 89 Federation components. Naturally,
things are not going smoothly everywhere. Whom can
the NDR rely on today centrally and in the regions?

In V. Babichev’s opinion, the time has come for
painstaking, systematic work, without haste and without
the desire to make an impression on admirers by
citing impressive figures. It is much more important to
strengthen the movement's ranks organizationally and
find true enthusiasts. That is why the NDR, to use
Viadimir Stepanovich’s expression, is “cultivating” an
aktiv —- in the villages, rayon centers, and cities big
and small. The main thing is to create organizations,
cells — even of only five or six people each.

“Unless we become a real mass movement,” V.
Babichev says, “there are no prospects.”

...Here it is perhaps worth stopping and returning to
what is, frankly, a very well-worn theme, but without
which no account of “Our Home” and its future would
be complete. Namely, the fact that it has been the
universal custom to call the NDR a party from the
outset. But could “Our Home” actually become a party
in the long term?

“It could,” my interviewee replies, “but that is in the
future...”

And he explains: In order to move on to a qualitatively
new status and become a more notable political force,
certainly every movement must eventually develop into
a more structured organization setting more serious po-
litical objectives. But today the question of reorganizing
the NDR movement is not at issue, V. Babichev stresses,
it is not relevant on the eve of the elections. This is
also the principled stance of our movement's leader,
V.S. Chernomyrdin. Creating a party would present no
great difficulty. But the NDR does not intend to “build”

PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES

yet another Garden Ring party. The basis of a party
has always been grass-roots regional organizations and
mass support. When that exists, it will be possible to
announce the creation of a party. The NDR already
has more than 300 professional functionaries in vari-
ous cities and villages, engaged in the creation of “pri-
maries” — the word is bureaucratic, but that is not
the point. Things are moving. In Volgograd Oblast,
for instance, NDR organizations have been set up in
all rayons, and grass-roots components are being suc-
cessfully established in Lipetsk, Samara, and Leningrad
Oblasts.

Were there mistakes in the setting up of the NDR? Yes,
and plenty of them, V. Babichev admits. Contrary to
the theory and practice of party building, it originated
from above, not from below — as if by order. The
present leadership of “Our Home,” in the shape of the
executive Committee, speaks quite calmly and frankly
about this. Ultimately the NDR is now on an upswing; it
has (judging by the results of the State Duma elections)
10 million supporters and 6,000 activists in the regions.
In the December elections the movement only made a
prediction, a trial: Is there a prospect of creating a viable
political structure on its basis? There is, but it must be
remembered that the objective conditions, like rungs on
a ladder, cannot be skipped; everything takes its own
uume, V. Babichev notes again and again.

Elections were held in Yekaterinburg this week to
the [Sverdlovsk] oblast and city Duma, and in my
conversation with V. Babichev I could not ignore their
results. Some 70 percent of the votes went to supporters
of the reformist course, incleding representatives of the
NDR and of local public organizations that had united
on its basis.

Only recently a favorite topic for political scientists
was the battle of the two “strong men” of Sverdlovsk
— Eduard Rossel, the present governor, and Aleksey
Strakhov, leader of the NDR regional organization. The
fight for the post of governor was surrounded with
gripping details to suit every taste, and among other
things “Our Home” was often castigated for supposedly
influencing the continuing conflict.

In real life everything turned out much simpler. There
was no NDR branch actually in existence in Yekater-
inburg in the summer of last year, at the height of the
showdown over which of the Urals “duo” was the wor-
thier, and so the movement could not have excried any
serious influence, whether “plus” or “minus.” Basically
Rossel and Strakhov, who have no major differences in
their understanding of reforms and democratic values,
were fighting for personal power. “It is absurd to speak
of any decisive participation by the NDR in those elec-

This report may contain copynghted maternal Copying and dissemination
1s prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

10 PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES

tions, it is far-fetched,” V. Babichev says, commenting
on last summer's situation.

The latest elections are another matter. Here the NDR’s
‘staff workers” talked calmly with the Sverdlovsk
Oblast leadership and with its opponents. After all,
there was a real threat: If you engage in imaginary
rivalry in the local authority elections, you might let
in the contenders running on a Bolshevik platform.
Common sense prevailed and a compromise was found
for strategic purposes.

Whereas in the Urals political life is seething with
activity, Certain governors are in a hurry to declare: We
are outside politics! The leader of the NDR executive
committee regards this as devious. No governor or head
of administration is outside politics. Their very position
in society and in a given region makes them the biggest
politician in their home oblast, kray, or republic. And
now more than ever before each of them must define his
position clearly: What political line do they hold, how
do they conceive of the economy, what do they intend
to do in strategic terms?

“Unfortunately, both reformism and democracy in the
new Russia began with extremes, with destruction,
extremism, radicalism,” V. Babichev observes. "So even
today not all the forces that cooperate with the NDR
primarily for tactical reasons fit into the ideas held by
the movement's leaders on the future of the movement.
Nonetheless it is open to cooperation with everyone.
Except, perhaps,” V. Babichev stipulates, “the CPRF
{Communist Party of the Russian Federation}. And even
then, it would be possible to hold a discussion with them
on some points.”

{Batygin] “And with Boris Fedorov, judging from his
recent speeches?”

“We were cooperating with him even before his mem-
orable speech at the recent congress of the All-Russia
Movement for Public Suppor for the President,”
Viadimir Stepanovich replies. “We are in constant
contact. If you break down the tasks into tactical and
Strategic, we are set to cooperate with virtually the
entire spectrum of political forces, with all parties and
movements, except for those who are trying to drag us
backward.”

[Batygin] “And with Yavlinskiy? Apparently all the
NDR’s links with him are severed. What happened, did
the ice pack break up and the ice floes move off in
different directions...”

{Babichev}] “Categorically, no! It is too soon to draw
such conclusions. Stating loudly today that ‘we have
gone our separate ways, like ships at sea," is absolutely

FBIS-SOV -96-080
24 April 1996

wrong, you should pay no attention to political rhetoric
— life takes its course. Time will tell'

I addressed my last question not to Babichev the
politician, but simply to a man with a rich experience
of life. Attributes of the past are returning to Russia
today. There is the decision to hold a parade in Red
Square, the return of the Banner of Victory, the holiday
that is being arranged under a presidential directive for
Heroes of the Soviet Union and holders of the Order otf
Glory. I asked my interlocutor: What does the return ot
these symbols to our life mean to him pessonally — a
step backward, or, on the contrary, another step toward
accord in society?

"I hope,” he replied, “that both the parade and the
Heroes’ meeting will remain forever and turn into a
good tradition. Or rather, this is not a new tradition, it
is Maintaining what our people had grown accustomed
to. Let us have the Red Banner of Victory. I do not think
we need talk about a return to the past. After all, in this
way we are paving the way to equilibrium, to accord in
our society, to realism in life, to all that is part of our
life, our flesh and blood. ‘Russia Is Our Home’ supports
this, I am sure, and everyone will regard the decisions
adopted in the right light, with understanding.

Russia: Exchange Starts Bids on Election Results

MK2404061696 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
23 Apr 96 pl

{Report by Maksim Kashulinskiy: “The Exchange Has
Determined the Rating of Presidential Candidates. Gen-
nadiy Zyuganov Is in the Lead, Whereas Boris Yeltsin's
Shares Are Going High”) ¢

[FBIS Translated Text} Russian futures exchanges,
whose main instruments now are contracts on state
short-term bonds and dollar exchange rate, in ‘:e quest
of new spheres of activity, have recalled that not only
exchange rate of securities and foreign currencies but
also political situation is liable to change. Yesterday
the Russian exchange started to trade in rated futures
contracts on the results of the first round of the
presidential election.

The starting bids were based on percentages obtained
in the public opinion poll conducted by the All-Russia
Center for the Study of Public Opinion [VTsIOM] in the
period of 4-10 April. It should be said that the results
of yesterday's bids differed rather significantly from the
Starting rates (percentage obtained in the poll multiplied
by 1,000). The greatest changes in starting rates were
observed with respects to contracts on Yeltsin (from
1,800 up to 2,525) and Lebed (from 1,000 down to 480)
According to the Russian Exchange analytical service,
some 500 deals were made yesterday in total

This report may contain copynghted matenal. Copying and dissemination
ts prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

Such bids — somewhat exotic for Russia but common ia
Western countries — are planned to be held daily right
till 14 June. Since by yesterday morning the Central
Electoral Commission had registered seven candidates
for president, for the time being only seven contracts
will be on sale. Each contract will have a conditional
denotation containing the first letter of a candidate's
name. As new participants in the election run are
registered, new financial instruments will emerge.

Participants in the bids are brokers, accredited to the
Russian Exchange's department for futures deals on
financial instruments. In addition, a broker can act as
a mediator and make deals on behalf of any interested
voter. As for the contract on the results of the first
round of the presidential election itself, it is largely
similar to other futures contracts. Trading is not a usual

PRESIDENTIAL ELECTION ISSUES 11

bookmaker’s game; it allows to profit from resale of
contracts.

A participant who is making a deal and is acting as a
seller feels sure that in reality the percentage of votes
to be given in the election for some specific politician
will be lower than the one specified in the deal; a
buyer, on the contrary, hopes for a higher percentage
to be received by his people's choice. Based on the
totality of deals concluded during one trading session,
a rated price of the contract is calculated, which will be
changing daily. Should a participant in the trading keep
his obligations and retain the contract till 14 June, the
amount of his actual gains/losses will be determined as
a difference between the rated percentage of votes to
be received and the real voting results for a presidential
candidate.

Russia: Russia-PRC Trade Eyed on Eve of Yeltsin
Trip

MM2404092796 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI

in Russian 23 Apr 96 p2

{Report by Georgiy Kunegin: “The Leaders’ Intentions
and the Realities of Life"]

(FBIS Translated Excerpt] In recent years new political
relations have developed between Russia and China.
Or, as the diplomats and politicians say, the desire
for a constructive partnership oriented toward the 21st
century has become a reality. [passage omitted]

After the breakup of the USSR, the niches that became
free were almost instantly filled by China's other trading
partners: Japan, the United States, the Southeast Asian
countries. And the negative effects were soon felt. The
reduction in trade turnover was halted only in 1995, but
the figures are depressing. Russia's share is something
like 1.9 percent, and we are the seventh biggest trade
partner. It is also worrying that the share of Russian
exports accounted for by machines and equipment has
fallen from 40 to 20 percent.

What is the reason for this? Aflter all, as China's
XINHUA agency states, “the two countries’ economies
are complementary.” The problem is this.

Countries trading with China enjoy state support over
a very wide spectrum in the exportation of goods
and equipment. From credits and bank guarantees to
judicial arbitration. Whereas Russian enterprises, joint-
stock companies, and individuals have to “sink or
swim” by themselves. But border trade and “shuttle”
traders are one thing, and large-scale orders for the

modernization of industrial enterprises and deliveries of

equipment are another. In the fifties and sixties we built
a lot in China, and our neighbors are now expressing
the intention of enlisting Russian specialists in the
reconstruction and modernization of national economic
facilities. But the problem is this: Without state support,
Russian entrepreneurs are simply out of the competition.
Especially since a process of gradual abandonment of
the barter system is now under way. Barter declined by
one-third in 1995 compared with 1994.

The new turn in Russian-Chinese relations is attested
by the fact that following the ratification of the border
agreement in 1992, the demarcation of the border began.
As of today, 98 percent of the border has been legally
defined. In the course of the demarcation in the Maritime
Kray region, doubts were expressed on two sectors. It is
a question of a border zone with an area of less than 10
square kilometers in Ussunyskiy region [kray] near the
Granitnaya River, where there is neither housing, nor
national economic facilities, nor agricultural land. And

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 13

the second sector is part of the marshy lett bank of the
Tumannaya River. Assertions that Russia is giving up
the Tumannaya estuary, which, incidentally, 1s 20 km
frvin this sector, are totally unfounded.

There is also quite a lot of speculation about the
“Tumangan” [as transliterated] international cooperation
zone project, which is only just beginning to take shape
and should not affect Russia's interests

Something else is more important: The Maritime Kray
authorities and the Chinese side are discussing the
question of the transit of Chinese freight through the
port of Zarubino, which should be boosted by the
construction of a railroad branch in this region, which
is nearing completion. It is also proposed to ship some
300,000 tourists per year from South Korein to China
via Zarubino. The benefit to Maritime Kray is obvious,
but will become a reality only if the border demarcation
question is resolved.

Among other promising areas of Russian-Chinese co-
operation there is the construction of a pipeline from
Irkutsk Oblast to China. But that is for the future, since
the project requires substantial capital investments and
the participation of third countries in the region.

And now Boris Yeltsin's next official visit to China ts
due to take place 24-27 April. XINHUA notes that “the
Russian president's visit and talks with Chinese leaders
will give new impetus to the constructive partnership
and cooperation between China and Russia.’

Judging from the program alone, one can say that our
president will have plenty of “work =m do. In three
days he is to talk with PRC President Jiang Zemin
and PRC State Council Premier Li Peng, local leaders,
and representatives of business circles. And, of course
numerous contacts with ordinary Chinese, who always
take an interest in Russia.

The packed program also includes the signing of more
than 10 joint documents on cooperation in the power
industry and ecology, the creation of a new mechanism
for trade and economic ties, and the protection of
intellectual property. But two documents hold a special
place on this list.

It is proposed that at a meeting in Shanghai of the
presidents of Russia, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Tajik-
istan, and the PRC, an agreement will be signed on
confidence-building measures in the military sphere
along China’s border with the four sovereign states. This
1s a unique document for Asia: For the first time, the five
countries are concluding an agreement on confidence-
building measures in a border zone with an area of
750,000 square kilometers, which is equivalent to the
entire territory of several Asian states. Yet only recently

This report may contain copynghted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

4 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

this 7,500-km border was one of the tensest in the world.
The agreement provides for briefing one another on ex-
ercises and troop movements and sending observers,
and in future some reduction in the troop formations
billeted along the border of Russia, Kazakhstan, Kyr-
gyzstan, Tajikistan, and China.

During Boris Yeltsin's visit it is planned to sign a
declaration on international questions and cooperation.
Its essence is the two countries’ vision of the present
era, ways of building a new, just, rational international
political and economic order on the planet, and the role
of world organizations in this.

Russian President Boris Yeltsin is expected in Beijing.
And it is apposite to recall the ancient Chinese proverb
which states: Good will should not be hidden in a dark
room.

Russia: Energy Projects With China Given ‘Top
Priority’

MM2304115596 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
in Russian 20 Apr 96 p 3

(“Own Information” report: “River of Oil and Gas in
21st Century Will Start in Siberia and Flow to Beijing,
Shanghai, and on..."]

{(FBIS Translated Text] We have just learned that the
Russian Federation Government has approved the draft
of a special agreement with the PRC. The agreement
pertains to “joint cooperation in the energy sphere.”

This agreement envisages the construction of oil and gas
pipelines from Russia's East Siberia region to China,
as well as the passage down these pipelines of energy
sources bound for third countries on the Pacific coast.

But the loudmouths and age-old opponents of President
Yeltsin and Premier Chernomyrdin should not rub
their hands in glee as they speak about the latest
“despoliation” of Russia. This draft agreement envisages
the “exploration, development, and operation of gas and
gas condensate fields on PRC territory...." In addition,
joint studies will be conducted in order to determine the
parameters of cooperation in the sphere of electricity
generation.

If you consider that such fuel and energy giants as
the Gazprom Russian Joint-Stock Company, the Inte-
grated Energy System Russian Joint-Stock Company,
the Krasnodar Kray and Irkutsk Oblast administrations,
and the Ministry of Fuel and Energy are involved in
these projects, it is easy to guess that cooperation in the
energy sphere between our countries is being given top
priority.

Two years has been allocated for formulating the
feasibility studies for the gigantic project. However,
it will be possible to begin construction work without
waiting for the final results and begin work on any of the
areas envisaged in the project as the feasibility studies
are made ready.

Russia: INTERFAX Comments on Yeltsin’s Visit to
Khabarovsk, PRC

L.D2304170696 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1552 GMT 23 Apr 96

[From the “Presidential Bulletin” feature]

(FBIS Transcribed Text] President Boris Yeltsin, now
on his way to visit China, arrived in Khabarovsk on
Tuesday where he is expected to stay until Wednesday
afternoon.

Our correspondent accompanying the Russian leader
reports that Yeltsin is scheduled to meet regional
_ Officials, visit the reconstruction site of a bridge across
the Amur river and get involved in a wreath-laying
ceremony at the local war memorial.

Before he flies out to Beijing at about 3.30 p.m. on
Wednesday, the president may visit a chapel put up to
the memory of the victims to political reprisals.

Speaking on arrival in Khabarovsk, Yeltsin said the
country’s presidential campaign was in full swing. He
spoke of presidential proxies, campaign headquarters
and support groups in his favor being set up throughout
Russia. “Canvassing is under way on the ground as well
as an advertising campaign,” he said.

On his planned tours, the Russian leader said next would
be very hard for him because of 16 trips he would have
to make to different parts of the country.

Yeltsin announced bringing a Far Eastern development
plan up to the year 2005 with him saying he would
endorse it there and then if no objections were raised
during a meeting with regional officials.

Many in Khabarovsk say the plan endorsed by the
Russian government will probably be given the status of
a presidential program. Yeltsin is also expected to sign
a number of documents including a decree on a federal
program of support to strategically important regions in
Russia's Far East.

Regional! officials say the program is caused by the need
for fuller incorporation of Russia’s eastern parts into the
dynamically developing economies of the Asian Pacific
region.

The status of a federal program will help the specified

regions towards priority budget allocations including
foreign loans and credits to implement their plans.

The program envisages a package of measures to
develop sparsely-populated regions of the Far North and
set up a large industrial center, Komsomolsk-Amursk-
Solnechnyy, on the basis of local defense and mining
businesses and related research centers.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 1S

Earlier, the economics ministry was appointed to coordi-
nate the program, with departments concerned expected
to implement it in specific directions.

The defense and energy industries dominated the agenda
of a meeting of regional officials and industrial man-
agers on Tuesday attended by First Deputy Prime Min-
ister Vladimir Kadannikov, Fuel and Energy Minister
Yuriy Shafrannik, Railway Minister Gennadiy Fadeyev
and First Deputy Economics Minister Andrey Shapo-
valyants, the regional administration's press office told
Interfax.

It was pointed out that until recently, about 60 percent
of the regional workforce and half of the regional
industry were involved in the production of defense-
related wares. Many of the defense-related plants which
are situated in local towns are brought to a standsull
leading to rising tension in the region.

The region is badly affected by a lack of its own
energy sector while the high cost of coal production
and railway shipments make all of the regional products
uncompetitive.

Participants in the meeting were unanimous that a well-
conceived government program of support for the Far
East is needed rather than separate federal programs

Defense Chief Pavel Grachev who is also part of the
government delegation met military district comman-
ders and visited several army units there

The Russian minister will report Yeltsin the results of

Romania is the only country in Central and Eastern
Europe with which Russia has not signed a political
treaty. Talks on this topic have been going on for four
years.

include a wording in the text of the document on the
need for overcoming the consequences of the Molotov-
Ribbentrop pact remains a stumbling block for the
signing.

As a result of this pact, the Soviet Union received
Bessarabia and Northern Bukovina in 1940, the terri-
tories now belonging to Moldavia and Ukraine.

In this connection, the Russian side regards as inexpe-
dient to include a provision into a bilateral treaty, which
directly affects the interests of third states.

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] STRASBOURG, April 23
(Itar-Tass) - “The recent summit on nuclear security in

Russia: Summit Discussions, Results Reviewed

MM2304114396 Moscow TRUD in Russian
23 Apr 96 pp 1,6

(Commentary by Aleksandr Ptashkin and Anatoliy Re-
pin plus “Postscript” by Vissarion Sisnev: “The G-7
Cannot Do Without Russia. This Was Demonstrated
Once Again by the Moscow Summit" }

{[FBIS Translated Text] Strictly speaking, the G-7 is
not an organization but an informal club of the top
leaders of the United States, Japan, Germany, Britain,
France, Canada, and Italy. They meet for a couple of
days once a year to discuss the world’s problems in
broad terms. Instead of decisions they make statements
in the form of declarations of intent which do not impose
strict commitments upon themselves, let alone other
countries. But as the members of the G-7 club occupy
dominant positions in the world’s finances and economy
and possess huge military potential and global political
influence, their meetings set the tone for those aspects
of the development of the international situation which
the G-7 discusses. In this regard, the Moscow meeting
was perhaps more significant than previous ones.

Starting with the fact that it was held outside the normal
timetable (the next regular G-7 meeting is due in Lyons
in the summer) and was devoted to a single special
topic — nuclear safety. The format of the meeting
was broader than the “seven.” For tbe first time the
Russian president took part in it from start to finish
and, what is more, in the capacity of cochairman, so in
this case a G-8 summit was already at work. Moreover,
the EU was represented in the person of European
Commission President Jacques Santer, while Ukrainian
President Leonid Kuchma was invited to the session on
the afternoon of 20 April, when the Chernoby! situation
was discussed.

The participants in the meeting quickly reached agree-
ment on the agenda topic, adopting a declaration which
makes safety a priority in the development of atomic
energy for peaceful purposes. It was this which was
stressed on the eve of the 10th anniversary of the Cher-
noby!l catastrophe. The document reflected what Boris
Yeltsin had urged in his speech of welcome to the sum-
mit: The need for environmentally clean and econom-
ically profitable nuclear technologies with absolute ad-
herence to the principles of safety, reliable guarantees of
the nonproliferation of nuclear weapons, and prevention
of the illegal circulation of nuclear materials. The latter
question will be jointly taken up by the special services
of Russia, the United States, and other countries. A dif-

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 17

ference in the approaches of Washington and Moscow
to Russia’s supply of peaceful nuclear technology to
Iran was noted during discussion of these problems.

The intention of the G-7 and Russia to sign at UN
headquarters this fall an international treaty completely
banning all nuclear tests is encouraging. For the treaty to
become all-embracing in nature, however, it is essential,
as the Russian Federation president pointed out, that
the PRC agree this step: Since France, after provoking
a barrage of protests, recently completed its series of
explosions in the Pacific, China remains the only power
which has not abandoned a program of further tests.

However, the Moscow summit statement can scarcely
wholly reassure a public worried by problems of nuclear
safety. Leonid Kuchma’s report on Kiev's decision to
close down the Chernobyl AES [nuclear electric power
station] by 2,000 caused absolute satisfaction. It is now
up to the West to fulfill its promises to allocate Ukraine
$3 billion in different ways. Quite a number of ticklish
problems raised at a meeting of public organizations
on the eve of the Moscow meeting were left outside
the ambit of the meeting. For example, its participants
ignored the position of military nuclear plants, including
those on nuclear submarines, which have come to the
end of their service life and the state of storage facilities
containing military radioactive waste.

The pressure of environmental campaigners on govern-
ments will scarcely slacken much after the Moscow
meeting. But given that all the blame in these ques-
tions is often pinned on the Russians alone, it is not
superfluous to note the Gpinion of President Clinton ex-
pressed at the news conference on 20 April: According
to him, the problem of nuclear safety, including reactor
safety, affects not just Russia — it exists in other coun-
tnies too — while the Russians have, the U.S. president
is convinced, made considerable efforts in recent years
to enhance the reliability of their AESs.

The leaders who assembled in Moscow were forced
to divert their attention away from the main topic of
the meeting and react promptly to the armed conflict
in the south of Lebanon. Their first session, on Friday
evening, was almost entirely devoted to this situation.
The summit participants came out in favor of the
swiftest possible cease-fire. Yevgeniy Primakov and
Warren Christopher flew to the conflict region, where
French Foreign Minister Herve de Charette was already
present, so (a very rare event!) the five-hour talks
between Yeltsin and Clinton on Sunday were held
without their chief diplomats.

Judging by the presidents’ news conference, the situa-
tion in Chechnya and Lebanon, questions of NATO ex-
pansion, disarmament, and other topics were discussed

This report may contain copynghted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

18 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

at this swift Russian-American summit, for which the
U.S. president specifically stayed on an extra day in
Moscow. As regards NATO's eastward expansion, the
Russian Federation and the United States maintained
their previous positions, but both leaders did agree that
“NATO expansion will not be accelerated for a while.”

The presidents did not ignore the Chechen conflict
either. Boris Yeltsin expressed confidence that the peace
settlement plan proposed by him is being carried out
and will be it plemented. Bill Clinton observed that the
United States “regards Chechnya as part of Russia” and
has always advocated a political solution of the problem.

No matter how important the topic of nuclear safety
is or how pressing the questions raised at the bilateral
talks between Clinton and Yeltsin are, all this was in
one way or another discussed against the backdrop of
the election campaign in Russia. Clearly mindful of the
fuss generated recently in the U.S. press over leaks
about Clinton's promise to support Yeltsin during the
elections, allegedly made during their meeting at Sharm
al-Shaykh, the U.S. president painstakingly sought to
avoid any direct expression of preferences. When asked
about the Russian elections, Clinton stressed that this is
a matter for the Russians themselves and expressed his
confidence that the “Russian people will look forward
rather than backward." But at the joint news conference
with Yeltsin on Sunday, when the Russian president
voiced his unshakable confidence that he will win,
Clinton, who was sitting alongside him, showed by the
whole expression on his face that he agreed with his
friend Boris’ aspiration. It cannot be ruled out that some
people in Washington will try to create a fuss on account
of this....

Regarding his meeting with certain leaders of the Rus-
sian political opposition at the U.S. Embassy, Clinton
explained it as follows: It is customary for U.S. presi-
dents to hold such meetings during visits to democratic
countries. Russia is a multiparty country, therefore he
meets several figures simultaneously.

Be that as it may, the Moscow summit demonstrated
the ‘attention paid to Russia by the leading world
powers and Russia’s role in the international arena. The
meeting's host sought to ensure that the actual setting
of the summit — the sessions were held in the Russian
Federation president's restored residence — strikingly
symbolized renewed Russia. The malachite, gilt, crystal,
and carpets enraptured the eminent visitors. Clinton's
Oval Office is really quite unlike Yeltsin's. Is that not
why it proved awkward to raise the question of new
loans?

But as regards Russia's obtaining full membership
of the club of the richest and most powerful, the

FBIS-SOV -96-080
24 April 1996

luxurious setting of the presidential residence did not
help: Moscow will again take part in the next summit,
in Lyons, as it did in Halifax, according to the 7 plus
1 formula. Boris Yeltsin has bluntly said that he is not
happy with this. If it is a question of Russia’s solvency
(the rich are always being asked for something), then
in terms of giving credit to foreign countries, above all
the CIS, Russia more than meets this condition of entry
to the G-8....

Postscript: Our correspondent Vissarion Sisnev reports
from Washington.

The American press cites Thomas Cochrane, representa-
tive of the Washington-based Council for the Protection
of Natural Resources, who gives the following assess-
ment of the summit: “Russia's Ministry of Atomic En-
ergy is the winner as regards problems of safety. It has
succeeded in postponing the closure of nuclear reactors
which cause alarm.”

But the general result was summed up by President
Clinton as follows: "We have taken yet another step
back from the nuclear abyss.”

Russia: Summit ‘Devalued’ by Leaders’ Focus on
Domestic Concerns

MM2304092896 Moscow KOMSOMOLSKAYA

("Summit Postscript” by Sergey Maslov: “The Seven
Stull Waiting To Admit the One to Their Ranks” }

(FBIS Translated Text] Our people do not fly across the
ocean to visit a bakery store. But the U.S. President's
mysterious American soul can permit him such an
amusing diversion. Bill Clinton's Russian voyage was
shrouded in mystery. The President was clearly in no
hurry to come to Moscow. He spent more time seeing
the sights of St. Petersburg and its environs than on the
solution of the global problem of the present day which
was the main topic of the summit conference — the
problem of nuclear safety.

The President with his American directness first wanted
to see some of the sights and... some bread. That is why
he popped into a bakery store on Nikolskaya Street

Bread, of course, is vital to us all. But according to the
Russian custom there should be some salt as well to
welcome a guest. Where was it? What was the essential!
ingredient of the event which (insofar as journalists were
allowed access to it) created the impression not of being
a G-8 or even a G-7 with a few pluses or minuses but of
a high society gathering. It is obvious that many of B
Yeltsin's guests could not get away from their domestic
daily problems. British Premier Major was hoping that

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

the visit to Russia would enable him to clarify relations
with Clinton over the “unduly warm reception” afforded
in the United States recently to Anthony Blair, the leader
of the Labor Party, the main opposition party in Britain.
The gentleman from London's 10 Downing Street was
able to use the platform of the Moscow nuclear safety
summit to call for... the abolition of the EU embargo on
the export of British beef. As far as could be judged
from the information that filtered through, Japanese
Prime Minister Ryutaro Hashimoto was almost more
concerned about the South Kuril islands which are under
Russian jurisdiction than about the Russian radioactive
waste dumped in the same sea off the shores of his
country. German Chancellor Kohl had an opportunity

German press even advised him: The best thing that
he could do this time for friend Boris would be not
to say anything but to keep silent. There was a special

Jacques Chirac, who recently provoked a worldwide
allergy by his stubborn determination in the pursuit
of the national nuclear test program. To be blunt, this
figure of yesterday's sinner transformed overnight into
a righteous man did not inspire any special trust as
cochairman of a summit on nuclear safety.

The documents produced by the meeting — the dec-

on a comprehensive nuclear test ban, a statement on
Ukraine, and a program to combat the illegal circula-

approaches to the solution of the problems, of course,

appearance of the summit as an unconditional success.
Hence the abundance of generalities and vagueness.

The main political factor which left its mark on the
course and results of the meeting is, of course, the
election of the Russian head of state, which is due in
eight weeks’ time, no matter how much Yeltsin might
deny it. Consciously or not this theme was part of the
overall picture of the discussions and one way or another
distracted their participants from the main issue. Thus
the nuclear safety problem itself was devalued in the
public's eyes. This is an example of how short-term
political interests can harm the most noble idea.

It seems that the treaty on a comprehensive nuclear test

latter, as is well known, holds a special position on this
question. Even before the summit began many observers
were bewildered: Why was it not possible to invite a

representative of the fifth nuclear power — if only as

was left unanswered.

Russia: Moscow Summit’s Results Questioned

MM2304110396 Moscow SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA
in Russian 23 Apr 96 p 3

[Article by Vasiliy Safronchuk: “Prestige in Abeyance.
What Did Russia’s President Gain by Cajoling the G-7
in the Kremlin?"]

(FBIS Translated Text] Over the last few days the
attention of Russia's and the world’s public was drawn
to the meeting of the heads of state and government
of the seven most developed countries in the world
and Russia. Taking part in it were Presidents Jacques
Chirac of France and Bill Clinton of the United States;
Chancellor Helmut Kohl of Germany; Prime Ministers
Ryutaro Hashimoto of Japan, John Major of Britain,
Jean Chretien of Canada, and Lamberto Dini of Italy;
and President Boris Yeltsin of Russia. The summit
of the G-7 with Russia's participation was devoted to
an extremely important problem — to ensure nuclear
safety. As is usual for such occasions, the leaders’
meeting was preceded by painstaking work by diplomats
and experts who prepared the necessary documents in
draft form. This is only naturai, bearing in mind that
the [leaders’] talks lasted just a single day, they held
only two sessions and hardly had an opportunity to state
their positions. B. Yeltsin presented a nine-points plan
approved at the 10 April session of Russia's Security
Council.

The meeting resulted in the adoption of four docu-
ments: 1) Declaration on the outcome of discussions
on the problems of nuclear safety; 2) Program for the
struggle against illegal circulation of nuclear materi-
als; 3) Statemeni on readiness to conclude a compre-
hensive and complete nuclear test ban treaty; 4) State-
ment on the Chernobyl AES [nuclear power station).
Despite all their significance, the importance of these
documents ought not to be overestimated. It must be
borne in mind that the G-7 meetings are informal and
any decisions adopted at them are recommendations and
wishes rather than international treaties and agreements
which are binding on all parties. Practice has shown
that many of the G-7's recommendations remain noth-
ing but wishful thinking. The first document is indeed
only a statement of intention by the meeting's partici-
pants to develop broad international cooperation in the
elaboration [razrabotka], production, and use of new,
safe, and economically advantageous nuclear power en-
gineering facilities. They also advocated the expansion
of nuclear-free zones and the development [razvitiye] of

is report may contain copynghted material Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

20 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

new technologies for inventorying and storing nuclear
materials.

But Russia's main proposal in the sphere of ensur-
ing nuclear safety was not taken into consideration.
The plan approved by the Russian Federation Security
Council contained an important provision — the nuclear
weapons of all nuclear powers ought to be concentrated
within the boundaries of their own territories. This pro-
posal was turned down by the G-7 and was not in-
cluded in the declaration. Furthermore, at his news con-
ference Chancellor Kohi declared Germany's negative
attitude toward the idea of a nuclear-free zone in Central
and East Europe. In practical terms this means that the
United States retains the opportunity to deploy missiles
with nuclear warheads on the territory of Germany and
other NATO members. Taking into account the plans to
expand NATO via Central and East European countries,
this creates a threat that U.S. nuclear missiles may ap-
pear right next to Russia's borders. Meanwhile, this year
Russia will complete the withdrawal of its nuclear ar-
senals from Belarus and Ukraine and their dismantling.
Thus, U.S. nuclear weapons will get closer to our bor-
ders while Russian weapons will be pulled further back.
This is a gross violation of the principle of equal secu-
rity which underpins the SALT I and SALT II treaties

The second document — the program for the strug-
gle against illegal circulation of nuclear materials —
provides for collaboration between Russian and West-
ern special services in this sphere. It also provides for
the elaboration and implementation of a system for de-
tecting possible secret nuclear activities. This program
is fully in line with the interests of Western states,
which are extremely worried by the possible leaks of
nuclear materials from Russia to so-called rogue coun-
tries (meaning Iran, Iraq, Pakistan, and others). It is
well known that the Western press regularly carries
planted stories about the smuggling of weapons-grade
plutonium from Russia. We recall last year’s sensational
story about the arrest of two smugglers at Munich Air-
port, allegedly trying to smuggle weapons-grade pluto-
nium from Russia. Later on it emerged that this “con-
traband” had been planted by...the German special ser-
vices. Think of how much is being written to the effect
that, due to shortage of funds, the Russian Ministry of
Atomic Energy nuclear stores are poorly guarded, that
poorly paid Russian specialists, who are experiencing
material hardships, are interested in exporting and sell-
ing nuclear materials abroad, and so on. Prior to the
Moscow meeting, U.S. Senator Nunn declared in his
statement to a Senate committee: “For the first time in
history we are encountering the collapse of an empire
which had 30,000 nuclear charges. Coupled with orga-
nized crim>, this poses a top priority problem for U.S.

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

security.” And CIA Director John Deutch, addressing a
Congressional committee, said: “... The Russians simply
lack the proper financial resources to ensure the safety
of their nuclear military complexes and adequately effi-
cient inventorying of stored nuclear materials, creating
the danger that nuclear materials could be moved else-
where.” It was no accident that a correspondent raised
this question at the joint news conference on the sum-
mit’s results held by B. Yeltsin and Jacques Chirac. B
Yeltsin was forced to offer explanations and assurances
that, from the viewpoint of nuclear safety, Russia “is in
a good state.”

The third document — on the need to conclude as soon
as possible, by next September, a comprehensive and
complete nuclear test ban treaty — could only have
been welcomed had it not been for two “but's.” First,
it does not take into account Russias wish to preserve
the so-called small-yield laboratory explosions. Second,
it has not been signed by the fifth nuclear power —
China. Meanwhile, it is well known that China continues
to conduct nuclear tests and would like to reserve
the right to conduct nuclear explosions for peaceful
purposes. India also insists on this. True enough, B
Yeltsin promised to “work on” the Chinese leadership
during his forthcoming visit to China this week with a
view to persuading it to join the agreement on nuclear
tests. But since this agreement was reached bypassing
China, B. Yeltsin is hardly likely to succeed. If the G-7
leaders really wanted China to join the agreement on

Moscow as an equal partner. This question was raised
by journalists, but the G-7 leaders let it be understood
that China itself should have asked to be invited to the
Moscow meeting. But this would have been an obvious
breach of diplomatic protocol. If the G-7 had been really
keen on its participation, China should have been given
an Official invitation from the meeting's cochairmen B.
Yeltsin and Jacques Chirac. Beijing prizes its prestige
As a matter of fact, to judge by everything, China — in
contrast with Russia — is not rushing to join the G-7. It
values its independence and does not need “recognition
by this Western club, aware of its own ideological and
political incompatibility with its present members

The agreement on the Chernobyl AES can only be

boosting his own prestige as world leader and gaining
the West's support ahead of the forthcoming presidential

election. True enough, the Western leaders emphasized

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 21

pro-Arab, and the pro-Israeli U.S. plan. They are similar
insofar as both provide for an immediate cease-fire
on the basis of the verbal agreement on nonviolence
