Grachev answered that his speech in the Duma was
misinterpreted, in particular in the Itogi television pro-
gramme.

He claimed the programme's commentator “has turned
everything upside down.”

Russia: Yeltsin Approves Juridical Status of
Russo-Chinese Border

LD2404091096 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English
0732 GMT 24 Apr 96

{By Anatoliy Vostokov, Andrey Shtorkh]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text} KHABAROVSK, April 24
(Itar-Tass) — President Boris Yeltsin stressed the im-
portance of a juridical status given to the Russo-Chinese
border on the Russian Far East, speaking in Khabarovsk
prior to departure to China.

“For the first time in nearly 300 years of our relations
with China we have a fully demarcated and juridically
regulated border,” Yeltsin said at a meeting with repre-
sentatives of the public today. “I think you still remem-
ber the times when China claimed 1.5 million square
kilometers of the Russian territory. Now this problem
has been removed,” Yeltsin said.

However, he noted that there were some problems left
in connection with the border and primarily, in the
Ussuriysk and Khasan regions of the Maritime Territory.
The president said that the concern of the population of
the Maritime Territory was understandable, but stressed
that international commitments must be fulfilled. The
length of the border line running across the territory of
these two regions is nearly 19 kilometers. If the border
is not demarcated here it means that the entire 4,000
kilometers of the eastern part of the border will be
called into question. It will be the Russian Far East that
will suffer then both politically and economically, the
president said.

Yeltsin said that he believes his task and the task of the
federal authorities is to look for disentanglement and
compromise, but noted that “there were cases when we
would not agree to a compromise, and in particular, on
problems of territorial jurisdiction of the three islands:
Bolshoy Ussuriysky and Tarabarov islands on the Amur
river in the proximity of Khabarovsk and the Bolshoy
island on the Argun river in the Chita region. Our stance
remains invariable as far as these islands are concerned.
The border line should remain where it runs at present,”
Yeltsin said.

Speaking on economic problems of the Russian Far
East, Yeltsin outlined fundamentals of the program
for long-term development of the Russian Far East

is report may contain copynghted matenal. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV -96-080
24 April 1996

and the Baykal region. He noted that the program of
the development of these regions was orientated at
huge investments expected to reach 370 trillion rubles
in the period of 10 years. The budgetary allocations
will account for one-fourth of all investments with the
State disbursements to the region expected to create an
incentive for investments provided by other sources,
including from abroad.

The president noted that the Far East has good opportu-
nities to become Russia's gateway to the Asian-Pacific
region. To open these gates and overcome former iso-
lation from neighboring countries is a task of strate-
gic importance. “Therefore, I attach great importance to
the development of relations with our powerful neigh-
bors — China, Japan, Korea, the United States, Canada,
and other countries connected with the Pacific Ocean,"
Yeltsin said.

Economic Issues
Russia: Livshits Interviewed on Economic Priorities

964E0957A Moscow INTERFAKS-AIF in Russian
25-31 Mar 96 No 12, pp 1-3

{Interview with Aleksandr Livshits, assistant to the RF
president, by special correspondent Boris Grishchenko;
place and date not given: “Russia's Isolation in the Risk
Space"}

[FBIS Translated Text} Aleksandr Livshits, assistant to
the RF [Russian Federation] president, is known by
sight from his press articles and television appearances.
Now, however, this influential economic scientist is
popular even in places that the capital's newspapers do
not reach. By order of RF President Boris Yeltsin, his
assistant has become the coordinator of the nationwide
“battle for wages.” Someone aptly nicknamed him
the “human economic rights representative.” Having
accepted this “title,” A. Livshits saddled himself with a
very complex task requiring additional expenditures of
time, which an assistant to the head of state is always
very short of. Nevertheless, A. Livshits made time for
the weekly INTERFAKS-AIF, answering questions by
special correspondent Boris Grishchenko.

[Grishchenko]} In your opinion, what is now the main
characteristic of the economic situation in Russia?

{Livshits} The main thing now is to maintain the existing
Situation, ruling out the destabilization of the domestic
economy and, at the same time, making decisions that
would advance economic ft form.

To maintain the situation means to maintain the basic
financial parameters, the budget, and the amounts of the
moncy supply and to prevent the growth of inflation and
the further decline in production. The changes that have

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 49

occurred recently are connected with the fact that, in
contrast to last year, the RF President, immediately after
the publication of the annual message to the parliament,
began a certain correction of the economic policy,
keeping in mind the priority task—to create reliable
guarantees for people's social and economic rights. This
dictates the present “battle for wages,” pensions, and
special payments to citizens from the federal budget.

{Grishchenko}] What complicates the situation in the
economy?

{Livshits} Of course, the approaching elections. In any
country elections give hardly anything to the economy.
For example, it is known that political scientists spe-
cializing in U.S. affairs never study the administration
policy six months before presidential elections and half
a year after them.

Because six months “before that" one cannot understand
anything owing to the election hullabaloo and half a year
later, if the former head of the administration retains
his post, he begins to make good on his preelection
promises. If, however, he loses his post, the newly
elected president begins “to correct the mistakes” of
his predecessor. In other words, during the first half
year after elections, in practice, the president does not
conduct his own policy.

To complicate the preelection situation, including in the
economy, Russia has its own special reasons (see the
diagram on page 3). It is easy to detect the difference in
the size of the zone of risk connected with elections—
ours and those of the United States. Unfortunately, ours
is bigger.

For example, the recent decisions adopted by the State
Duma on the Belovezhskaya ay;cement convince us
of this. According to my belicf, this step by the
lower chamber buries the illusicn, which has still
flickered in society, that, allegedly, the communists are
simply recruiting (which is natural) their electorate,
but they will become completely different when they
receive power. We must be realists. The communists
set for themselves the task of denouncing Belovezh
agreements, and they fulfilled it. Consequently, they will
just as persistently fulfill all their other tasks. Thereby,
the space of risk for the country is expanding in the
direct sense of this word.

{Grishchenko] In your opinion, what can happen in the
economy if the communists come to power?

[Livshits} Anything is possible. Here is a figure, which
has not yet been published. According to expert data,
during past years foreigners’ demand for shares of
Russian privatized enterprises was estimated in billions
of dollars. For the first half of this year (that is, until

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

SO RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

June), the total collection of statements of intention
amounts to approzimately $350,000. As they said at one
time, thanks to the party for our happy childhood!

In this connection I ask myself the following question:
Who in Russia will directly benefit from the artificial
confrontation of political preelection vectors? I do not
find any. An everyday conversation, which I had with
a person who voted for the communists in the elections
of the State Duma, comes to mind. He said openly: “If
I had earnings or income of $2,000 a month, I would
vote for Boris Yeltsin in June.”

In my opinion, this person intuitively has arrived at
the conclusion that by no means everything in present
Russia is hopelessly bad. Simply, he is chronically short
of money, and it seems that other problems do not worry
him, including the problem of the infamous deficit, from
which many generations of our fellow citizens have
suffered.

[Grishchenko] Perhaps you came across a not quite
typical representative of the communist electorate?

{Livshits} It seems to me that the program, with which
the communists are going to the elections, in fact,
does not correspond to the expectations of many in
the communist electorate itself. I am not sure that this
circumstance will not have the most unexpected impact
on the communists in the course of the elections.

If they keep telling their potential voters that everything
is absolutely bad in Russia now and that nothing at all
has come out of economic reforms, such a position will
not suit many.

{Grishchenko] In what do you see contradictions here?

{Livshits} For me personally the communists’ quite
innocent, at first glance, phrase that, in case of their
victory, they will support in the economy everything
that was done according to law—let, they say, no one
fear anything—seems the most dangerous.

kt should be remembered that the new Russian economy
was created in a totally empty legal space. Everything
in it that was not filled by law, naturally, relied on the
president's edicts. If we follow the communist logic,
their economic policy, when they come to power, will
begin with the revision of edicts.

{Grishchenko] In your opinion, in what spheres will the
first steps be taken?

{[Livshits}] For example, the edict on privatization. In
fact, an indisputable redivision of property will begin.
After all, all privatizaton was built on presidential edicts,
beginning with vouchers and ending with mortgage
auctions.

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

(Grishchenko] It is unlikely that millions of citizens,
who do not own large property, will want this...

{Livshits} However, there was also an edict on free
trade. This means that, if there is a great desire, it will
be possible to bring virtually the entire RF population
to account on the basis of the article “speculation.”

On the same grounds, it is possible, if there is an urge,
to institute criminal proceedings against the majority of
fellow citizens for a violation of the law on currency
operations. If you exchanged $100 for rubles, this is
a crime. Thus, it seems to me that it is premature to
talk about the solidity of the economic interests of the
present communist electorate.

{Grishchenko] However, the majority of Russians are
ready to follow those who promise them a better life in
the very near future...

{Livshits} We do not promise, we act, but they do
not let us... Please recall the infamous contest for
the purchase of the shares of the Russian Svyazinvest
Joint-Stock Company by one respectable Italian firm.
The deal, which unexpectedly did not go through last
fall, could have brought Russia $1.5 billion. | would
like to reproduce part of my dialogue with a Western
entrepreneur after the unsuccessful contest. A detail of
no small importance: This occurred in January.

“Why was the contest canceled when everything was
ready for a deal and ihe benefit seemed obvious and
mutual to the parties?"—I asked the person | was
talking to. “There are technical details, which must
be discussed,” the partner answered, adding that this
“discussion” required half a year. To my suggestion
to accelerate the rates of work and to do everything
necessary, for example, in four months, he shook his
head: “We will not manage to do this before July...

Whom does Russia and Russians have to thank for the
loss of $1.5 billion? Perhaps Gennadiy Andreyevich
Zyuganov?

One and a half billion dollars is a sum, which, converted
into rubles, covers 100 percent the debts of the federal
budget on wages to power departments. Who took
away this money from soldiers and officers? Not those
politicians, who in words so zealously worry about
the security of the state and society? This is what is
distressing: Servicemen couid have already received
this money personally. The requisites of accounts, to
which we were about to transfer these funds, were
ready. We were about to, but did not, transfer them
This Svyazinvest project deal, which did not take place,
is only one example. Unfortunately, such examples
which complicate the factor of the implementation of
economic policy, are not decreasing. Now, however.

This report may contain copynghted matenal. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV -96-080
24 April 1996

Russian authorities are ready for changes in people's
economic behavior under the effect of the preelection
situation.

{Grishchenko] In what is this readiness expressed?

{Livshits} We are ready, in particular, for possible
changes in dealers’ plans on the state treasury bond
market. We are ready for a rush or an increase in
the demand for currency on the part of citizens. We
are ready for those troubles connected with the effect
of other destabilizing factors capable of affecting the
Russian market, which has not become stronger.

{Grishchenko] In case of the communists’ victory in the
elections, is a return to the previous economic model
possible?

{Livshits} First, I consider this question purely hypo-
thetical. To reply to it, there can be only one answer:
A mechanical return to the former “socialist model of
management” is no longer possible in Russia.

Another thing is possible. If we indulge in fantasy and
for an instance visualize the communists’ victory in
June, | can say with full confidence that the party of
inflation, not the Communist Party, will come to power.

[Grishchenko] What kind of party is this?

{Livshits] In its composition this party is much broader
than the Communist Party of the Russian Federation. It
includes sizable forces, which support the Communist
Party precisely because of its infic:tonary orientation.

The Communist Party of the Russian Federation is infla-
tionary in its very essence. One can hardly imagine that,
if Gennadiy Andreyevich (Zyuganov—INTERFAKS-
AIF) finds himself in the presidential chair, he will an-
nounce in his program speech: “Financial stabilization
is my goal.”

No, he will say: “An upsurge of industry and the
restoration of the national economy are my goal...” He
simply cannot have another way out except to proclaim
large-scale projects.

However, to rapidly raise, for example, industry, money
is needed. It can be taken from two sources—either from
the Central Bank or from the federal budget. Of course,
it is possible to take it from the population, but our
fellow citizens have long been afraid of everything. |
think that, in case of an appeal to them, rather they will
try to buy something valuable “as a reserve.”

Let us examine the situation further. It is impossible to
take anything from the Central Bank because there is a
law and there is Sergey Dubinin. It is also impossible to
take anything from the federal budget because there is
am appropriate law or the federal budget. Consequently,

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 51

the first steps of the new government could be directed
toward a change in laws on the federal budget and the
Central Bank.

[Grishchenko] Are there no legal ways of changing such
laws?

{Livshits}] At first glance such steps could even seem
natural and politically acceptable. They would not cause
an open rejection in the world, as, for example, the
transition to the rationing system, or something in the
same spirit, did.

However, an increase in the deficit is the essence of the
replacement of laws because it is no longer conceivable
to increase taxes, nor does it seem possible to lower
budget expenditures.

{Grishchenko] What wiil be in such a case?

{Livshits} During the first months after such steps,
prices, perhaps, will not even jump high. After all,
the anti-inflationary potential established by the present
government is quite solid. This March with its 2.5-
percent inflation confirms this. On the other hand, a
large budget deficit will be revealed further, and direct
crediting through the Central Bank will begin because
the state treasury bond market is virtually oversaturated
and there is nothing left to borrow there.

If we further analyze such a situation, the IMF will be
the first to react to it (I assume that such a reaction
is also predicted by communist analysts). They will try
to convince people: Finally, we have thrown off the
running knot of the IMF. However, getting rid of this
“running knot" will cost us not only $4 billion in 1996
alone. Russia will manage, although with difficulty, such
a sum.

Such actions would certainly bring about our rupture
with the Paris club. Such a course would certainly cause
nonresidents’ departure from the state treasury bond
market and would result in a ban on the floating of
Russian bonds in Europe.

{Grishchenko] What general damage could be done to
the country if its leadership carried out such a thing in
practice?

{Livshits}] According to preliminary calculations, the
total “price” of this is $22-24 billion in the current
year alone. All the currency reserves of the Russian
Federation [RF] are half of this and are earmarked for
other purposes. Therefore, if such an alternative arises,
the West will want, first of all, to get back its money. It
makes absolutely no difference to it what wil! happen
in Russia. We are alone in the face of our own choice,
of the risk imposed on young Russia by the opponents
of reforms.

This report may contain copynghted material. Copying and dissemination
ts prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

$2 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

{Grishchenko} However, one can try to resist the dictate.
We have already had such historical experience...

{Livshits} It seems that Zyuganov is trying, as far as
possible, to repeat it, maintaining that “we will not start
begging on our knees for mercy from the West.” The
West's response will be unequivocal—seizure of our
accounts. Taking into consideration that the currency
reserves of the RF Central Bank are abroad, first of
all, the country will be deprived of all its currency
reserves. Now this is done very efficiently through the
court. Everyone understands what will be later. I do not
want to use the word “disaster,” especially as we have
analyzed by no means the most probable situation. I
suggest that we return to present Russian realities.

[Grishchenko}] Some economists now come out in
favor of improving the Russian economy through an
inflationary stimulation of demand. What do you think
about this?

{Livshits} I do not reject these ideas 100 percent. Russia
has already arrived at the plateau of low inflation, where,
in principle, such a step is possible. In life, however,
low inflation is still very unstable. In my opinion, such
heating up, nevertheless, is premature.

Incidentally, this heating up can be carried out only
if there are agreements with international financial
organizations, which control Russia in a weekly regime.
The debts of the former Union hang like a heavy load
on the country’s neck.

I would like to recall that the IMF represents not so
much money as a symbol of the fact that it is possible to
deal with a specific country and to restructure its debts;
in brief, about debts. In 1991 the debt of the former
USSR totaled approximately $70 billion. Now it is $120
billion. However, the RF has no special problems with
the $50 billion that it owes. Russia is paying all the
interest and repaying the principal part of the debt.
This is a normal debt, which many countries have. The
previous load is another matter. It really weighs down
on it, representing a great danger for the economy of
the new country.

[Grishchenko] In your opinion, what is the future of
mortgage auctions?

[Livshits] | was present at the beginning of these
auctions. Their very idea seemed promising to me. From
the point of view of daily life, it is understandable,
simply because what one cannot sell one should not
keep in vain.

However, it is impossible not to admit that from the very
beginning the plan for holding these auctions was, to
put it mildly, rather weak. No wonder the RF President

FBIS-SOV -96-080
24 April 1996

had to especially issue two edicts literally after the first
plan, to give five or six orders to the government, and
to remove a number of enterprises from auctions. Even
after this the auction plan cannot be considered ideal.

I know only one thing: We now direct all those who
were involved in auctions toward resolving disputable
questions through the court.

{Grishchenko} How do you see the fate of the “cur-
rency corridor,” which is so much being discussed and
debated?

({Livshits} Everything will depend on a purely economic
Situation, without any political admixture. It will be nec-
essary to see how by 1 July domestic inflation behaves,
if we will be able to retain tariffs on the output of natural
monopolies, what will the export dynamics be, and so
forth. It will be very important to understand whether
we have fully suppressed inflationary expectations on
the currency market. If we are able to do all this by the
summer, I do not rule out that the “currency corridor”
can be replaced with some other alternative, for exam-
ple, an “inclined corridor,” when the Central Bank will
regulate the currency rate in strict accordance with, or
with a small difference with respect to, the dynamics of
current inflation.

However, no matter what alternative is adopted after
1 July, I want to disappoint those who expect that the
removal of the “currency corridor,” allegedly, will bring
about an immediate crash of the ruble and lead to the
restoration of the previous “export Eldorado.”

[Grishchenko] What is now the role of the former
first deputy prime minister, Anatoliy Chubays, in the
formation of the course of the Russian economy?

{Livshits}] Chubays left the government, but not politics.
He has good contacts with business, and he gives
recommendations to many politicians, including the
president's assistants.

{Grishchenko] And to you?

{Livshits} To me also. Incidentally, | receive recommen-
dations from hundreds of people. Many telephone and

Aa RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Russin: Soskovets Details Privatization ‘Errors’
14M2404092096 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
in Russian 20 Apr 96 pp 1, 2

[Article by First Vice Premier Oleg Soskovets: “Priva-
tization: Working on the Errors”]}

(FBIS Translated Text] There is no element of our re-
forms that plays on people's minds more than privatiza-
tion. What results has it produced so far? What lessons
must we learn from it?

The first stage of privatization mainly entailed a redis-
tribution of property. Which explains its “breakneck”
pace. In 1993 around 43,000 enterprises were privatized.
If you compare January of that year with this January,
there were seven times more enterprises privatized then.

The crazy pace gave rise to errors. I consider that
the most important was the sale of enterprises at their
book value, which had not been reviewed for a long
time. It would often happen that enterprises would go
for a price comparable to that of a new, exclusive
apartment. The second mistake was that closed-type
joint-stock companies were set up on the basis of
privatized enterprises, even though they were created
using public assets. And finally, the third mistake was
to have an imperfect mechanism for managing state

property.

We should perhaps resolve this particular problem to-
day. At present Goskomimushchestvo [State Committee
for the Management of State Property] appoints a state
representative to the board of directors on the recom-
mendation of the organ of executive power. But experi-
ence has shown such a system to be completely ineffec-
tive. One and the same person usually serves as a repre-
sentative to three or more companies and cannot under
these circumstances either resolve specific problems or
determine industrial policy. A proposal was made at the
recent session of the Association of Financial and In-
dustrial Groups council that state representatives should
work on the basis of trusteeship contracts. And each
should work at only one enterprise. What a sound pro-
posal!

Audits, which are a powerful means of monitoring
directors, are supposed to play an important role in
resolving the problems of state ownership. But what is
happening at present? After the auctions had been held,
the system of departmental monitoring was dismantled,
enterprises were put at the disposal of directors, and the
ministries were left without funds or staff.

Today some journalists like to label everyone who
talks about defending state interests and ownership as
reactionaries and conservatives. However, no one has
yet abolished national and state interests.

FBIS-SOV -96-080
24 April 1996

A graphic example of this is found in our experience of
the privatization of defense complex enterprises. The
military-industrial complex’ distinctive features were
not taken into consideration. Moreover, a decidedly
standardized approach was applied to the defense sector.
The increased pace of the sale of shares determined their
low value in advance. While the book value of property
has increased more than a thousandfold, the price of
shares compared to their face value has not increased
more than thirtyfold. More than 80 percent of shares
sold have ended up in the hands of companies that either
do not want to or canaot invest in high-tech industries.
On the other hand, they are able to block decisions to
issue new shares to attract new capital.

At the first stage of privatization, the defense industry
considered that it could survive only by splitting up
into its constituent parts. This was the worst thing to
do. Design offices are incapable of existing without
a plant. Now a sobering-up process has taken place:
It is time to create new, integrated structures. The
Kompomash and Informatsionnyye Tekhnologii holding
companies are being set up. Already in operation
alongside these structures are such companies as Antey
(military and refrigerator technology) and the Russian-
Belarusian Vympel, which brings together Russian
ABM defense science and Belarus’ production base.

Arguments are raging about the status of defense
complex enterprises. Some propose renationalizing
the military-industrial complex. I think that here we
need to study the experience of countries with market
economies, where the state either buys or sells shares
in enterprises, depending on the economic situation.
Take France, for example. Even more interesting is the
U.S. system, where there are no part-state-owned or
State enterprises at all. But at the same time there is
a fiercely competitive struggle for state orders and a
system of monitoring their execution.

We still have to make the choice. But in the meantime
a 13 April presidential edict created a Federal Com-
mission To Guarantee Control Over the Manager cnt
and Privatization of Defense Complex Facilities, which
should change the situation in our military-industrial
complex.

We should, of course, not now start taking things
easy. The most difficult task will be to ensure a high
return for the treasury from privatization on the one
hand and to stimulate investment activity on the other
Unfortunately, Goskomimushchestvo has so far failed to
give a clear outline for such a strategy or to to carry out
properly the assignments of the Commission for Current
Questions of Management, which constantly examines
the problems associated with this “golden mean.

report may contain copynghted matenal. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

Unfortunately, since the stage of “wholesale” check pri-
vatization, we have succeeded neither in obtaining high
returns for the treasury nor in attracting investment in
manufacturing or in its modernization. Neither citizens’
savings nor the financial structures’ resources have been
mobilized. The volume of foreign investment has even
decreased.

We should decide immediately the fate of the shares
which the state pledged the banks in 1995. Do you
recall how many ovjections the snap auctions spawned
back then? Everybody still remembers the passions
surrounding Norilskiy Nikel, the Yukos oil company,
and others. In connection with these pledges the problem
arises of settling with the agent banks, which must be
paid | September 1996.

The pledged shares include those of five major oil
companies. But, you see, according to the list confirmed
by the government, shares in «.ese oil companies cannot
be sold ahead of schedule. It is therefore necessary
to extend the period after which the agents have the
right to sell biocks of shares in the oil companies until
a special decision is made by the government and to
refrain from holding any shares-for-loans auctions for
other oil companies during 1996, extending the period
over which these and other strategically important
enterprises’ blocks of shares are assigned to the state.
At the same time measures to control privatization
deals need to be made tougher by making penalties for
violations harsher.

The time has come to draw up documents that guar-
antee support for domestic commodity producers. Af-
ter all, Russia is turning more and more into a raw
materials supplier. The proportion of GDP represented
by machine-building and agriculture has fallen sharply,
while that represented by trade and the fuel and raw
materials sectors has increased. But even the latter have
gotten into a difficult position because we are not fi-
nancing exploration work properly. It is essential to find
additional money for these purposes. Otherwise, there
will soon be nothing to extract.

As we can see, there are many problems. We must once
more draw the attention of Goskomimushchestvo and
the Economy Ministry to the necessity of expediting
the resolution of fundamental questions of privatization
here, while not forgetting the lessons of recent years.

Russia: Crisis in Defense Industry Seen Deepening
MK2404112896 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
24 Apr 96 p2

{Report by Yuliya Latynina under the “Defense In-
dustry” rubric: “Crisis of the Russian Defense Indus-

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 55

try Deepens. Defense Enterprises Owe 3 Trillion to the
Treasury")

(FBIS Translated Text] The defense enterprises’ perfor-
mance in the first three months of this year confirms a
deepening of the crisis in the Russian military-industrial
sector. According to Vitaliy Vitebskiy, deputy chief of
the Economy Department of the State Defense Indus-
try Committee, the overall production volume of Rus-
sian defense industry enterprises was 69.7 percent last
month against March 1995. Only the Voronezh-based
“Energiya” Science and Production Association reported
14.6-percent growth. In March 1996, aircraft production
at these enterprises was 67.6 percent of last March's
level; radio industry production, 64.7 percent; electron-
ics, 65 percent; ammunition, 61 percent. In March 1996
civilian production at plants under the State Defense In-
dustry Cozamittee’s jurisdiction fell to 70.4 percent —
and military production, to 68.4 percent against March
1995.

The press service of the State Defense Industry Com-
mittee reported that the defense enterprises’ debt to the
treasury has exceeded 3 trillion rubles [R]. At the same
time, as of 10 March, the state’s arrears in back wages
were R1.5 trillion — and in the payment for defense
orders, R4.8 trillion.

The defense industry's work force shrank by 14.1
percent in February 1996 against the same period of
1995. Meanwhile, the average monthly wage did not
exceed R490,000 per person (ranging from R345,000 in
the electronic industry to R670,000 in the shipbuilding

industry).

The practice of building closed towns or enterprises,
forming urban centers where over an 80 percent share
[of the economy] was produced by defense orders, has
brought about a disastrous social situation after the total
defense order was cut by many times back in 1992. In
such cities as Arsenyev (the birthplace of the “black
sharks" — K-50 helicopters) or Severodvinsk (nuclear
submarines), the actual unemployment rate is more than

60 percent.

The 1995 budget earmarked R1.4 trillion to finance five
federal conversion programs. In fact, only R250 billion
have been released.

At the same time, the question of privatizing, not just
converting, defense enterprises to civilian use remains
one of the most controversial issues. A considerable
number of plants would like to remain under state own-
ership. And the State Defense Commitice has pointed
to the purchase of shares in the “Saturn” joint-stock
company or the Moscow Helicopter Plant by foreigners

Russia: Government Panel Views Oi] Production
Decline

MM2204155196 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA
(“Business in Russia”
section) in Russian 22 Apr 96 pp 5-6

{Article by Vladimir Kucherenko: “Oil as a Factor in
National Security")

(FBIS Translated Text} Despite the slowdown in the
decline in extraction rates (3.5 percent in 1995 as against
10.2 percent the previous year), the situation is alarming.
According to the most optimistic forecasts from the
Ministry of Fuel and Energy, by the year 2000 it may
be possible to keep the extraction level within 285-295
million tonnes a year (the 1990 figure was 516 million
tonnes). However, a further sharp decline cannot be
ruled out.

These figures were cited at a session of the Government
Commission for Operational Questions which discussed
measures to stabilize oil extraction in Russia.

The decline in prospecting for new oil reserves and in
test drilling threatens to become the cause of a reduction
in extraction. Test drilling has declined by more than
two-thirds since 1991 (from 4.1 to 1.24 million meters
in 1996), reaching the levels of the 1950's. The growth
of oil reserves shrank from 810 million tonnes in the
year before the reforms to 150 million tonnes last
year. Geological prospecting work in promising new
territories — East Siberia and the Arctic and Far East
continental shelves — is practically at a standstill..

In the words of Roskomnedr [Russian Federation Com-
mittee for Geology and the Use of Natural Resources]
Chief V. Orlov, there is an immutable normative:
Prospecting — the growth of reserves — must exceed
extraction levels by 150 percent. This rule started to be
broken in 1989, and by 1991 the growth of reserves
equaled 206 percent of the level of extraction. Last
year’s results showed this figure struggling to reach 65
percent. And the amount of deep drilling come to a mere
one-third of the 1991 figure.

The reasons? The state has stopped financing the
geologists out of the treasury, providing only 6 percent
of the planned appropriations last year. And the big
oil companies’ resources for prospecting purposes have
been decentralized and become fragmented.

As a result we are witnessing the intensive depletion of
deposits. which are being operated on the “live now, pay
later” principle. Roskomnedr warns that oil producers
in the Ural-Volga and North Caucasus zones, where

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

deposits are 70 percent exhausted and the oil contains
a high percentage of water, can no longer increase
extraction.

West Siberia, with its Tyumen and Samotilor fields, also
needs emergency measures to save the raw material
base — the depletion level of resources there has been
calculated at 34.4 percent. On average Russia’s “black
gold” deposits are 44.3 percent depleted.

If this situation persists in the next few years, by the
beginning of the next millennium the volume of oil
extraction in the country could fall to 147-150 million
tonnes. Which would plunge us into the most severe
Crisis. For the Russian Federation Ministry of Fuel and
Energy has calculated the minimum oil extraction levels
acceptable for Russia — 241 million tonnes according
to the optimistic forecast and 259 million according to
the pessimistic forecast. If the rate of decline of “black
gold” extraction remains at last year’s level, crisis point
could be reached somewhere between 1997 and 1999.

The price would be inordinate. You see, food supplies
for the big cities, the stability of the consumer market
(where imports for petrodollars predominate), and the
stability of the ruble on the foreign currency exchanges
all depend at this time on oil extraction. Not to mention
heat supplies, the provisioning of the Army and the
Ministry of Internal Affairs, and Russia's levers of
influence in the international arena. A sharp decline
in oil extraction when the country is highly dependent
on imported goods could encourage separatist processes
and a struggle for shrinking sources of petrodollars and
fuel.

The situation can be rectified. In the words of
Roskomnedr Chairman V. Orlov, there are promising

and as yet poorly prospected zones in the Western

Urals and the Northwest Caucasus (Chechnya). In the
Moscow syneclise and the Mezenskaya zone. Prospect-
ing levels are low in Yakutia, Eastern Siberia, and
the Arctic and Far East continental shelves. Reserves
of 14.5 billion tonnes of oil are concentrated on the

Barents and Kara Sea coasts alone. Although the “easy”

Russia: Gold Mining, Reserves Policies Reviewed

964F1877A Moscow ARGUMENTY I FAKTY
in Russian Apr 96 No 16, p 13

{Article by Sergey Kalinin: “Why Our Gold Is the ‘Most
Golden’ in the World”]

([FBIS Translated Text} In the opinion of scientists, in
the past 3,000 years, mankind has mined around 100,000
tonnes of gold (the weight of two Ostankino television
towers). A certain part was irretrievably lost, some has
been preserved in museums, but the greatest part of this
treasure rests today in state storage facilities, in safes
of major world banks, and in the possession of private
owners. For many centuries, this “coveted” metal has not
lost its value, but even today remains as a measure of
wealth and power, and those who have large quantities
of it still rule countries and peoples.

The Reserve Does Not Strain the Pocket

Let us take, for example, the United States, whose gold
reserve is estimated to be 8,500 tonnes, and is the largest
in the world. It is carefully guarded by the U.S. Federal
Reserve System in Fort Knox. A like amount is held
in the safes of the International Monetary Fund, which
is located in New York. Germany owns 2,875 tonnes,
Switzerland—2,590 tonnes, France—2,545 tonnes, and
Japan—753 tonnes.

Some sources maintain that, in better umes, the gold
reserve of the USSR reached up to 3,000 tonnes

Ths report may contain copynghted maternal. Copying and dissemination
1s prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

58 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

However, it is impossible to verify these data, since
such information has been secret for the entire existence
of the Soviet Union. As of January 1996 (according
to information obtained from Roskomdragmet [Russian
Federation Committee on Precious Metals and Precious
Stones}), the gold reserve of Russia comprised 340
tonnes. Frankly speaking, that is not much. if we recall
that by natural reserves of this precious metal we occupy
third place after the RSA [Republic of South Africa] and
the United States and that there were over 10,000 tonnes
of it mined in Russia in the 20th century alone.

Comparing the last two figures, one cannot help but
begin to believe in the “special” path of Russia, with
its endless political and economic experiments, up to
the present day. After the civil war, gold and precious
metals were sold to combat the ruin and famine, and
then—for the industrial thrust into a bright future.
During the Great Patriotic War, we paid the Americans
in gold for their lend-lease shipments. During the crop
failures of 1961-62, we avoided bread lines by once
again bankrupting the state treasury. In the late 1970's,
gold in huge quantities was used to buy equipment for
developing the oil fields in Western Siberia. In recent
times, the United States has also been selling about 200
tonnes of gold annually, but their gold reserve is not
being depleted so rapidly.

For Every Sixth Sister—a Set of Earrings

In the early 1980's in the USSR, up to 60 tonnes of gold
were processed annually for the needs of the jewelry
industry, with only 26 narrowly specialized enterprises
engaging in this. In Bronnitsy, a suburb of Moscow,
for example, there was the only plant for making gold
chains in the entire Union. Perhaps many still remember
that this product at that time was one of the goods that
were in extremely short supply. The primary buyers of
jewelry during those years were the residents of the
republics of Central Asia and the Transcaucasus.

Since that time, the production of jewelry goods has
declined by five times. Last year in Russia, only 10
tonnes of gold were used for their manufacture. As they
say, a holy place is not empty for long, and, therefore,
this gap is being filled by numerous foreign firms, with
considerable profit to themselves. Thanks primarily to
them, one in every six women in Russia has been able
to adorn herself with a ring, a set of earrings, or a chain.
According to the data of the VNII [All-Russia Scientific-
Research Institute) of Yuvelirprom, the most popular
among our fellow countrywomen are lightweight goods
of S85 assay, weighing 1-4 grams (in 1995 there were
5 million such pieces sold). In Western Europe there is
one piece of jewelry for every fifth woman, but they
prefer more expensive products.

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

The relative share of export of jewelry adornments
from Russia is inconsequently small and is estimated
at only $10 million per year. The leading place in gold
processing, however, belongs to Italy. On the Alennine
Peninsula, they process around 500 tonnes of metal into
jewelry—almost one-fourth of the world’s mining.

Soon There Will Be No One To Tax

Since 1993, a persistent tendency toward reduction of
gold mining has been vit!ined in Russia. Last season
too, only 123 tonnes of gold were mined (according
to Roskomdragmet data), which is 10 tonnes ics» than
in 1994. Goskomstat [State Committee on Statistics]
cites an even lower figure—111 tonnes. According
to optimistic predictions, if such rates of decline are
retained, already in 13 years the mining of gold in
Russia on an industrial scale may cease. Pessimists,
however, believe that this will happen much sooner.

In previous years, the Soviet state bought up ail of the
precious metal, down to the last gram, from its miners
at the price set for them. In exchange, it generously
subsidized and protected its favorite “chicken that laid
the golden eggs.” During the war, Stalin personally
controlled the state of affairs in the sector. Yet the
current government, which has rejected subsidies, has
nevertheless in essence reserved its monopoly right to
dispose of the gold.

Well, so be it. The state does not give anything in return,
but it even takes the very last bit. In Russia for the
present day, the highest maximal rate of pay for use of
mineral resources is up to 10 percent of the volume of
mined gold (in the world this rate is one-two percent).
The total payments and taxes to budgets of various
levels exceed 50 percent of the proceeds received from
the gold that is sold. Even in more developed countries
with favorable climatic conditions, mining companies
pay the state a maximum of 35 percent, and not from
the total sale, but only from the profits.

Aside from the taxes, the direct expenditures for mining
the gold are also increasing, despite the fact that its
procurement price is remaining practically unchanged
Just since the beginning of the current year, the cost of
energy, fuel, materials and equipment has increased by
25 percent. In the years of stagnation, the most ordinary
domestic-made bulldozer cost the miner | kilogram
of gold. Today prospectors are forced to pay over 10
kilograms for it! Many gold mining enterprises found
themselves bankrupt, according to the results for 1995

In order to save the sector and make its products coim-
petitive, we do not have to make any great discoveries
It is enough simply to familiarize ourselves with the
experience of the countries that are very successful in

is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

this endeavor. In the early 1980's, few perceived Aus-
tralia as a serious competitor (only 25 tonnes of gold
per year). However, the preferential regimen of taxa-
tion, which exempted mining companies from taxes on
profits and payments for mineral resources for several
years, allowed it to become one of the world leaders,
with an annual indicator of 250 tonnes.

The situation is today developing according to a similar
scenario in Argentina, Chile, Bolivia, Nicaragua, and
Papua-New Guinea. Naturally, foreign investors zive
preference to them and to the countries of the CIS
(Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan, Tajikistan), but not to Russia.

Among the unfavorable factors that do not facilitate an
influx of foreign capital into Russia is the geographic lo-
cation of the primary gold-bearing regions (remote and
cold Kolyma, Chukotka, and Yakutiya), requiring addi-
tional capital expenditures for development of the in-
frastructure and operational and transport expenditures.
Moreover, the number of gold deposits discovered at
the present time in other countries is fully adequate to
satisfy the planet’s demands for many years to come.

Will There Be Enough Gold for the Commercial
Banks?

In general, the world can get along quite well without
our gold. But can our gold producers get out of the crisis
situation by themselves? The chairman of the Union
of Gold Industrialists, Valeriy Brayko, gives a positive
answer to this question:

“To start with, we must review the tax policy, create a
real market in precious metals, and not one declared
in words and on paper. Then not only foreign, but
also domestic banks will begin to invest money in
development of the sector.”

ht is true, in November of last year the RF [Russian
Federation} Government and the State Duma adopted

RUSSIA NATIONAL AF?'AiRS 59

documents on development of the market in precious
metals and precious stones. Specifically, they stated that
the state monopoly on gold and diamond mining must
be eliminated and that the state retains the right caly
to priority acquisition. But for the present time this is
all still theory, while in practice the situation is quite
different.

The government has set a quota on gold mining in the
amount of 145 tonnes for 1996. Of this, 131 tonnes must
go to the state fund, while the remaining 14 tonnes may
be sold to commercial banks. But will enough gold be
mined so that there will be any left over for unrestricted
sale? And, even if everything works out favorably, will
the 150 commercial banks that have licenses to buy gold
want to buy it? After all, they do not have the right to
export the gold abroad or to perform security deposit
Operations with it. And within the country, gold does
not have much liquidity at the present time.

And how can we speak of any market, if Russia does
not have a precious metals exchange and the effective
criminal legislation provides penalties for owning a gold
ingot or unfaceted diamond? Up until the present time,
our refining plants cast primarily only standard bank
ingots weighing from 12 to 13 kilograms, intended for
large-scale interstate accounting operations.

Not too long ago, Viktor Chernomyrdin was shown
experimental samples of ingots of various weights—
ranging from 1 gram to | kilogram—at Roskomdragmet.
Most likely, if the order is given to begin large-scale
casting of this “small change,” then it will be intended
for smail banks and primary buyers, that is you and
me. And this means that... But let us not get ahead of
ourselves. Time will tell.

Ukraine: Kuchma Calls For ‘Evolutionary’ NATO
Expansion

LD2304123796 Kiev UNIAN in Ukrainian

1100 GMT 23 Apr 96

{FBIS Translated Text} Strasbourg — “Ukraine attaches
great importance to the development of closer rela-
tions with NATO and the Western European Union,”
Ukrainian President Leonid Kuchma told a session of
the Council of Europe’s Parliamentary Assembly.

The Ukrainian leader said that, in view of the present
situation in Europe, Ukraine is seeking its own road
toward NATO. “Ukraine does not oppose NATO's
expansion, but sees it as an evolutionary and open
process which should not recreate confrontation,” the
president stressed. He said that Ukraine sees NATO's
future as a gradual transformation “from a defense
alliance into a Euroatlantic security structure embracing
all European countries.”

The Ukrainian president stressed that the creation of
a nuclear-free zone in this region would have a ma-
jor stabilizing effect on European development in ev-
ery aspect, including the politico-military one, as was
pointed out at the G-7 summit in Moscow. “While draft-
ing a future European security system, we should take
into account the interests of nonaligned neutral coun-
tries such as Ukraine,” Kuchma said. According to the
Ukrainian president, a continental security system would
be doomed to failure if such countries were to end up
in a so-called buffer zone. He called for an effective
mechanism to guarantee the security of nonaligned na-
uons.

course of strengthening its independence. “The people
of Ukraine chose their path in the referendum and will
continue to follow it,” he said.

Kuchma emphasized at the same time that Ukraine will
actively cooperate with neighboring countries on the
basis of equitable partnership, considering the creation
of suprastate structures “such as those stipulated in the
treaty between Russia and Belarus” to be impossible.

Leonid Kuchma believes that plans to revive the USSR
of create new structures of this type have no prospects
and that they “are not working.”

Expressing his opinion on the Chechen conflict, the
Ukrainian president noted that Ukraine favors the terri-
torial integrity of any state. At the same time, according
to him, it does not support the measures which Russia
is using to achieve its aim. Leonid Kuchma said he was
in favor of the soonest possible peaceful settlement of
the Chechen problem.

The Ukrainian president stressed the necessity of ob-
serving the Helsinki accords of 1975 when resolving
problems arising on the way to signing a comprehen-
sive treaty between Ukraine and Romania. “We cannot
infringe on Europe's existing structure,” he noted.

The head of the Ukrainian state said he is convinced that
the Black Sea Fleet problem will definitely be resolved
as soon as the issue of Sevastopol is resolved. The status
and conditions for basing the Russian Black Sea Fleet
are the main thing in this problem. he noted.

UKRAINE 61

Expressing his opinion on NATO expansion, Leonid
Kuchma said he is in favor of only democratic states
without territorial claims becoming new members of the
alliance. He also stressed that there should be no new
split of Europe into two blocs, so that a new Berlin Wall
cannot be built. “This should be avoided and the process
of NATO expansion should occur taking into account
the interests of all states, including Russia,” Ukrainian
President Leonid Kuchma emphasized.

As he reviewed the social and political situation in
Ukraine, President Kuchma said that democratic reforms
were continuing in the context of a sharp confrontation
of political ideas.

President Kuchma recalled Ukraine’s commitments
made after its admission in the Council of Europe and
said that many commitments drew a mixed response
from society, including political forces and state struc-
tures. For instance, he said that the problem of capital
punishment sparked vigorous debates and the idea of
its abolition was rejected by part of society.

“We are aware, though, that international legal commit-
ments will be international legal commitments and have
to be honored,” President Kuchma said. This means that
Ukraine will stick to the agreements stemming from its
membership of the Council of Europe.

Kuchma paid special attention to the problem of accom-
modating the deported peoples returning to Ukraine. He
said that his country bore no moral responsibility for
the crimes committed by the totalitarian regime against
those peoples and asked for international assistance in
accommodating the migrants.

The Ukrainian leader called on the PACE members
to speed up the ratification by their parliaments of
the agreements of partnership and cooperation between
Ukraine and the European Union, signed in 1994. “Our
Strategic goal is to become a full-fledged member of the
European Union,” Kuchma said.

As he dwelt on the problem of European Security,
President Kuchma said that at this point it would
make sense to get back to the idea of a political and
economic association in Central and Eastern Evrope of
States seeking integration with European structures. He
Suggested creating a nuclear arms free zone in Central
and Eastern Europe.

About NATO's expansion Kuchma said that Kiev saw it
as a “gradual, evolutionary and open process that would
cause no confrontation or conflicts.”

Ukraine sees future NATO as a purely defensive Euroat-
lantic structure of security for all European countries.

Kuchma spoke highly of the role of the Organization
for Security and Cooperation in Europe (OSCE) as
a “unique instrument of preventive diplomacy, the
settlement of critical situations and elimination of their
effects.”

Kuchma recalled that Ukraine cooperated closely with
the OSCE on the problem of Crimean autonomy. In that
connection he said that Crimea’s autonomous status was
about to be settled in keeping with the current legislation
and the Ukrainian constitution.

Ukraine: Nationalists Accuse Ministry Structure of
Antistate Goals

AU2404092596 Kiev NESKORENA NATSIYA
in Ukrainian 3 Apr 96 p2

[Statement by the Press Service of the Organization of
Ukrainian Nationalists in Ukraine}

(FBIS Translated Text] By an order of Ukraine's De-
fense Minister Valeriy Shmarov issued on 25 November
1995, a clandestine headquarters was created at the Min-
istry of Defense. Its objective is to draft and implement
a plan to destroy Ukraine's defense system and liqui-
date the sovereign Ukrainian state itself.

The members of this clandestine anti-Ukrainian and an-
tistate headquarters, hiding in Pushcha-Vodytsya [near
Kiev}, secretly developed their scheme for the system-
atic destruction of the army. The chief of General Staff
had no knowledge of this. Under the cover of so-called
reforms, they started to implement it.

Major General Leonid Vasylyovych Kravchuk, first
deputy chief of Ukraine's Armed Forces Academy,
revealed this on 3 February.

It was Colonel General Anatoliy Lopata, chief of the
General Staff. who revealed the information about

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

the creation and activities of the clandestine antistate
headquarters. For doing that, Defense Minister Shmarov
dismissed him. The secret plan envisages, in particular,
liquidation of the command structures of two military
districts and other military structures and formations
that were established over many years and on which
much money was spent. This may be interpreted as
the first step toward destruction of the defense system
that Ukraine has created with such difficulty over the
past four years. According to Leonid Kravchuk, some
chiefs of the Defense Ministry Main Directorate were
given very little time to read the project drafted by the
clandestine headquarters and were then ordered to sign
it. Refusal would have entailed very serious sanctions.
Unfortunately, there were quite a number of generals
ready to sign any document for the sake of maintaining
their privileges. Ukrainian officers and patriotic generals
regard the creation of this illegal structure within one
of the power ministries as an attempt at a military coup
aimed at the total destruction of the state's defense
system and then the liquidation of its sovereignty. Major
General Kravchuk called upon the officers corps to
take all steps to force the Defense Ministry Collegium
to resign en masse for the attempt to undermine the
state's defense capability, for the unsatisfactory combat
readiness of the army, and the inability to focus its
efforts on the immediate tasks of defense.

The main demands of the participants in the confer-
ence on the problems of building the Ukrainian National
Army include the immediate dissolution of the military
collegium, reevaluation of the officers corps, the return
of Kostyantyn Morozov [former defense minister} and
Volodymyr Mulyava [former military chief in charge
of servicemen's patriotic indoctrination] to their posts
at the Defense Ministry and the revision of those inter-
national agreements that do not accord with Ukraine's
national interests.

radioactive wastes and take up the task of providing

Ukraine: Environment Minister Does Not Rule Out
New Chernobyl! Leak

BR2404122096 Stockholm DAGENS NYHETER
in Swedish 21 Apr 96 pA6

{Interview with Ukrainian Environmental Frotection
Minister Yuriy Kostenko by Michael Winiarski in Kiev;
date not given: “Not Only Ukraine's Problem"]}

{[FBIS Translated Text] Kiev — “Everybody asks if we
are going to close down Chernobyl by the year 2000.
My answer is that it is not that simple and that a joint
effort from the whole world is needed. This is not only
Ukraine’s problem,” Ukrainian Environment Minister
Yuriy Kostenko said in an interview with DAGENS
NYHETER.

Kostenko especially cautioned that the so-called sar-
cophagus, the concrete shell that encases the wrecked
reactor, is not a guarantee against new radioactive emis-
sions.

On a few occasions Ukrainian President Leonid Kuchma
has said that he is prepared to close Chernobyl by the
turn of the century. In a meeting with G-7 leaders in
Decembder 1995 Kuchma more or less promised that
Chernoby! would be closed. But Ukraine lays down very
firm conditions for this to take place as early as the year
2000.

The Kiev government demands that the world’s richest
countries step in and pay nearly $5-6 billion which
supposedly is the cost of a closedown and to cover the
resulting loss of electric power.

[Winiarski} Is this a form of blackmail! that Ukraine is
conducting against the West: Pay, otherwise we wil! not
close it?

UKRAINE 65

{Kostenko}] The negotiations have only started. The
outcome depends on the compensation that Ukraine
gets. In the memorandum signed at the G-7 meeting in
December neither an amount nor a date was specified.

But just to stop operations would take 80-85 months
and cost billions of dollars. And that is still the least of
our worries. The overshadowing problem is the fourth
reactor which is inside the sarcophagus.

{Winiarski] Has a decision already been reached regard-
ing Chernobyl’s future?

[Kostenko} Our position is unchanged. We are prepared
to close it on the condition that we are compensated
for the social, energy, and technical costs which would
result from a closedown. There are 6,000 people work-
ing at the plant and Ukraine gets 6 percent of its elec-
trical energy from the Chernoby! plant which must be
replaced.

But the question is not only closing or not closing. In
any event it must become a safe plant. There are 200
tonnes of nuclear fuel in Chernobyl. And who knows if
there could be a new eruption of nuclear energy?

{Winiarski] How great is the risk for a nuclear reaction
in the sarcophagus?

[Kostenko] We must first establish how much concen-
trated nuclear fuel is inside the sarcophagus. During
these 10 years things have happened, but we do not
know exactly what: physical processes, nuclide shifts,
explosions. There is a real danger that a critical mass
could develop as a result of thermochemical shifts in
nuclear fuel. This in turn could lead to an eruption of
radioactive material.

We cannot draw any certain conclusions at this point
since we have not examined the contents of the sar-
cophagus in four years. Many people claim that a new
nuclear reaction is impossible. | would like to say to
them: Remember that the worst supposedly could not
happen earlier either, and still the Chernobyl disaster
happened. We cannot rule out anything.

{Winiarski] How safe is the sarcophagus?

{Kostenko] It is said that the shell will last for 35 years,
that is to approximately the year 2020. But we had an
earthquake in the area in 1991 and it is impossible to
rely on that it will last that long.

The truth is that we do not know. It is impossible to
spend a long time in the sarcophagus because of the high
radiation level. To avoid excessive doses of radiation it
iS necessary that hundreds of specialists relieve each
other, but there simply are not that many top level

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

66 UKRAINE

researchers in Ukraine, perhaps not even in Russia.
Therefore international help is needed.

{Winiarski} Thae are those who believe that it is
unnecessary to clase Chernobyl of all places since
the plant allegedly is Ukraine's safest after all the
investments in safety that have taken place.

[Kostenko} I do not agree. This is perhaps true when
it comes to operational routines and alarm installations.
But Chernobyl has high-power pressure-tube reactors
using graphite which have such defects in their basic
construction that they impossibly could be safer than
the more common light-water reactors.

[Winiarski} Is the sarcophagus without leaks today?

[Kostenko] There is a problem with leaks in the reactor,
cracks have developed, not only in the upper part.

But this is true not only of the shell. Large amounts
of radioactive waste have accumulated around the
sarcophagus and underneath it. This threatens two-thirds
of Ukraine's water supply. Half of the country’s surface
is already poisoned.

{Winiarski] Do you personally believe that Chernobyl
will be closed?

(Kostenko] Yes, sooner or later. But we must not only
close it but also handle the whole aftermath of the
disaster together. Yes, I believe it will be closed. But
not that all the problems thereby would be solved. Now
we are marking the 10th anniversary of the disaster. But
there will be a 100th anniversary, too. And the question
is what it will look like then.

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

Ukraine: Chernobyl Official: No Casualties in Fires

AU2304180396 Paris AFP in English
1713 GMT 23 Apr 96

{FBIS Transcribed Text] Kiev, April 23 (AFP) — Fire
swept Tuesday (23 April) through several villages in
an abandoned area around the Chernobyl nuclear power
plant, but there was no rise in radioactivity from the
facility, officials said.

There were no casualties in the outbreak, which was still
raging by mid-afternoon, said the official in charge of
the exclusion zone around the plant, Boris Turkovski.

The cause of the fire was not immediately known.

But Turkovski told AFP he thought it might have been
accidentally caused by villagers allowed to return to
visit relatives’ graves in abandoned villages the 30-
kilometre (18 mile) exclusion zone around Chernoby].

“They throw cigeratte ends on the ground and the grass
is exceptionally dry,” the official explained.

“The fires did not cause any increase in the levels of
radioactivity on the site,” the official said.

The villagers are allowed to return to their homes to
visit cemeteries after the Orthodox Easter.

Every year some 5,000 to 10,000 people are allowed to
enter the forbidden zone only at this time of the year.

One of the plant’s four reactors exploded almost exactly
ten years ago in the world’s worst civilian nuclear
disaster.

Belarus: Growing Support for Lukashenka
Analyzed

WS2304143296 Minsk BELORUSSKAYA DELOVAYA
GAZETA in Russian 18 Apr 96 p 5

[Article by Valyantsin Zhdanko: “’All of Us Trust You
So Much, Comrade Lukashenka — Perhaps Even More
Than We Trust Ourselves”]}

[FBIS Translated Text} In March and April Alyaksandr
Lukashenka managed to strengthen his already firm
position in the eyes of the Belarusian people. If elections
were to be held in the immediate future, some 57.8
percent of respondents would vote for him. This is the
highest indicator in the whole 20-month period of Mr.
Lukashenka’'s rule.

Perhaps only on the eve of the 1994 elections was
Alyaksandr Lukashenka shown such sympathy on the
part of the electorate. Since the last poll, which was held
by the “Novak” laboratory at the end of February and the
beginning of March [1996], the number of supporters of
the president's policy increased by 11.4 percent.

It is very likely that all the events which occurred at
the end of March and the beginning of April were ad-
vantageous to Alyaksandr Lukashenka. These include:
the suppression of participants in the 24 March rally;
the anti-integration action held by the opposition on
2 April; and pompous celebrations held in the Krem-
lin in connection with the signature of the Belarusian-
Russian agreement on the creation of the Community of
Sovereign Republics.

There is a widespread feeling among the Belarusian
people that nobody can compete with the president for
leadership. Zyanon Paznyak, who occupies second place
in the ratings, has the support of only 6 percent of

WESTERN REGION 67

respondents. It seems that his departure abroad after the
24 March events did not win him popularity.

Another reason for the growth of the nation’s love
toward Alyaksandr Lukashenka results logically from
the attitude of the majority of the Belarusian people
toward the idea of restoring a unified state and returning
socialistic achievements, and from the fact that they
are pinning their hopes on the president's promises. In
light of this, the loud speeches delivered by opposition
activists were advantageous for Lukashenka. There was
a real opportunity to show to the population the enemy,
who seems not to be entirely defeated, and is still raising
his head. For a considerable portion of the electorate, the
mass demonstrations protesting against Lukashenka’'s
policies became a visible answer to the question of
who is hampering the implementation of the president's
daring ideas.

The figures prove that these conclusions are not ground-
less. Almost 64 percent of those polled assessed as neg-
ative or “rather negative” the activities conducted by
the participants in the demonstrations. Only 20.7 per-
cent of respondents assessed these activities as positive
or “rather positive.” Over 48 percent of respondents sup-
ported — to a certain degree — the authority's activities
oriented toward suppressing the protest actions. A little
more than 34 percent of respondents negatively assessed
these activities. We have these results despite the fact
that the press and television carried photographs and
videos of violence against demonstrators involving the
use of batons and tear-gas....

At the first news conference held after the creation
of the Belarusian-Russian Community, Alyaksandr
Lukashenka exaggerated the degree of support of his
initiatives on the part of the population. The number of
those opposed to the rapprochement with Russia is not
at all a “handful of misled people.” However, in general
there are 48.1 percent of those who fully support the
“new level of integration” and another 27.5 percent of
those who are “rather supportive.” These figures are
more than convincing.

At the same time, the majority of respondents have
no illusions with regard to the state of affairs in the
economy and changes in the national economy. Some
50.9 percent of respondents stated that the situation
in the country did not improve during Alyaksandr
Lukashenka’'s rule.

Nevertheless, those people are once again ready to vote
for him. Why is that? For how much lenger will the
high rating of Mr. Lukashenka be based on blind faith,
without heing backed up by real improvemenis in the
economy and the social sphere?

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

oa WESTERN REGION

As a “real” national president, Alyaksandr Lukashenka
is striving to indulge the tastes, opinions, and sympa-
thies of the majority of people who are faith“ul to him.
This tactic, which is multiplied by his eloquence and
personal charm, yielded positive results for him not for
the first time.

We can dwell on this source for quite a long time before
the fantasy of authors of the next era-making initiatives
runs out, and as long as Belarus, although to a smaller
degree, still remains a sovereign country. Afterwards,
there will be a possibility to blame sovereignty and its
supporters for the failure of domestic and foreign policy.

Besides, one should keep in mind the main social groups
on whose support Alyaksandr Lukashenka is primarily
relying. Around 80.5 percent of people with elementary
education are ready to vote for Lukashenka again; as
well as 52.9 percent of people with secondary education,
and only 43.4 percent of people with higher education.

This material is based on the results of a national opin-
ion poll which has been held by Andrey Vardomatski's
“Novak” sociological laboratory between 30 March and
10 April 1996. Some 1,106 people were polled.

Moldova: Creanga Affair Reveals Top-Level Dissent

AU2404114296 Chisinau INFOTAG in English
1930 GMT 23 Apr 96

(“INFOTAG’s commentary: ‘Events Around Defence
Ministry Have Completely Exposed Differences Be-
tween Power Structures in Moldova’™]

{FBIS Transcribed Text} Events around the Moldovan
Ministry of Defence, which attracted the attention of
many both in the republic and outside it, have cast light
on some internal problems. Political observers could
only guess about their existence, but to speak openly
about them was not in local traditions. As a Moldovan
top-rank official put it, the republic has again appeared
at scandals pages in the world press, for the first time
since the 1992 armed conflict in Dniester

Despite the administration's efforts to “keep everything
at home” and avoid undesirable publicity after the split
which took place in the ruling Agrarian Democratic
Party [PDAM] after President Sregur left it. the events
around the defence ministry have completely exposed
the differences in the power structures

This report may contain copynghted matenal. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copynght owners

FBIS-SOV-96-080
24 April 1996

After Snegur has dismissed General Pavel Creanga from
the ministerial post, which caused a strong negative
reaction of the Parliament and government, it became
Clear that there was no more hope for a reconciliation
between the head of state, on the one side, and the
Parliament and government, on the other.

The minister's dismissal has become kind of a touch-
stone showing presidential powers (or, rather, their lim-
itedness) and the principal unwillingness of the ruling
party — in person of the parliamentary majority and the
government — to make concessions to Snegur after his
slashing criticism against them.

The new Moldovan Constitution (as well as most
laws), which was adopted in certain political conditions,
reflected the concrete situation and arrangement of
political forces at the moment.

It was adopted with a due account of the opinions
voiced by the Council of Europe and other international
organizations. The Constitution guarantees basic rights
and freedoms to Moldovan citizens. However, the basic
law is not totally free of drawbacks, which foreign
experts could not help noticing due to their insufficient
knowledge of Moldova, which was well in the interests
of the ruling majority.

The drawbacks are first and foremost due to the fact
that the Constitution was passed under the pressure of
one political party, with a full defiance of the opinion of
the opposition who took part in the constitution drafting
but chose not to vote for it, because they were unable
toi sence the final wording of crucial articles.

Such a dictatorship became possible thank to President
Saegur as well, who silently supported the PDAM the
parliamentary elections, and joined it after the party won
the elections. At those times, nobody could predict the
noisy split, as controversies with the PDAM leadership
were only emerging, and the president supported the
Constitution and signed it. thus having reduced his own
and successors’ plenary powers.

Seeking to bring some of the powers back, or at least to
look at the reaction of the new political opponents — his
recent allies, the President has subjected the government
to an unsparing criticism. It was, perhaps, valid, but one
and two years ago the Cabinet deserved it no less than
now.

After this verbal atiack, the head of state began threat-
ening the Government with resignation in case it fails to
fulfil his strict decree on clearing all wage and pension
arrears. And in order to demonstrate the resoluteness
of his intentions, Snegur dismissed the Minister of De-
fence. having skillfully used the shortcomings of the

WESTERN REGION 69

legislation which often fails to correspond to the Con-
stitution.

The president must have realized that even in case
of failure he would have earned a certain benefit and
demonstrated to the public that the current legislation is
so imperfect that the head of state is unable even “to
fire a corrupted official.”

Naturally, the problem was not in Creanga, and not in
his pension age, which the President was so particular
about. Corruption and various violations existed in the
Defence Ministry ever before Creanga, but they were
in the attention of only the opposition press. For an
example, one can refer to a dubious transaction of
selling Moldovan jet fighters to South Yemen.

A presidential ally from the first days of the national
armed forces formation, Pavel Creanga himself would
hardly oppose to the will of his supreme commander-in-
chief, the more so that Snegur first offered a “peaceful
resignation,” having promised to the general Moldova's
highest national award.

However, the situation developed in another direction
due to

Creanga's ambitions and the backing he received from
the government and parliamentary majority who decided
to “rescue the persecuted general” and, at the same time,
to let Snegur [word garbled] that he is supposed to
respect the opinion of the Parliament, elected by the
entire nation, and the government appointed by this
forum.

In such a situation, the power structures had nothing to
do but to apply to the Constitutional C curt and state their
readiness to yield to its decision, and thus to demonstrate
to the nation once again their adherence > democratic
and constitutional norms.

For Snegur, it was much more painful to keep his
word. The parliamentary majority has revised its once
very critical attitude to the Court, whose decisions were
heretofore of little pleasure to the forum. The Court
itself has kind of rehabilitated itself, and proved to
its critics that its decisions have always been in good
harmony with law and are free of political preferences
and outside pressures.

Meanwhile, the pressure on the Court was particularly
strong, though indirect. The media were referring to
“opinions of independent experts” without indicating
their names or addresses. A group of parliamentarians
addressed to the Council of Europe and, as became
known later, to the U.S. Congress. The judges could not,
naturally, remain deaf to Snegur's appeal to the General
Prosecutor's Office demanding to initial proceedings

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners

70 WESTERN REGION

against senior officers who refused to obey to the
supreme commander-in-chief.

A certain pressure was exercised even by Pavel Creanga
himself who stated his readiness to resign but only in
case the Court will establish the non-constitutionality of
the presidential decree.

It is noteworthy that after Creanga was reinstated in his
former position, he seems to have changed his mind,
and is not only refusing to retire, but is avoiding the
media.

As for the power structures, they are busy clearing up
their relations, and are resolute to defend their positions.
First the president sent an ultimatum-like message to the
prime minister demanding to nominate a new ministerial
candidate, after which he addressed to the Parliament
requesting to consider nomination of another minister.

The premier responded with a not-quite-very-polite
public refusal, and the Parliament is still silent, which
fact cannot be regarded as a consent.

FBIS-SOV -96-080
24 April 1996

In near days, the president is going to appear in
Parliament. Most probably, he will again use a rough
language against the defence minister as well as other
government members. It will be quite easy to forecast
the reaction of the Parliament and government, which
will be a logical continuation of the recent events.

Thus the “languid” differences of the past months are
acquiring an active character. And it cannot at all be
coincident that the Parliament is planning to pass the
Presidential Election Law at the second reading almost
simultaneously with Snegur’s appearance in the forum.

As the elections are approaching, the “war” between
the power structures will be unfolding. This “war,” with
its strategy and tactics being scrupulously worked out
by the sides, may bring any unexpected moves and
surprises, new allies and betrayers in either camp. Both
sides may use their “heavy artillery” in the form of
making public some compromising information. And,
like in any war, there will definitely be victims.

Kazakhstan: New Communist Leader Signals
Change

LD2404092996 Almaty DELOVAYA NEDELYA
in Russian 19 Apr 96 p 1

{Unattributed report: “Communism With a Speaker's
